% \textcolor{red}{A potential title: Safe, Cost-Effective Spatial Policy Learning With Applications to [Agricultural-Related Groundwater Contamination in Wisconsin]}
\begin{abstract}
%Mitigation of the harmful effects of contaminants in drinking water requires the implementation of policies that simultaneously satisfy regulatory limits and minimize implementation costs. For example, one of the most common groundwater contaminants is nitrate. Excessive nitrate in groundwater is commonly the result of fertilizer applications, sewage disposal, or leaky septic systems, therefore a common approach for reducing nitrate in drinking water is to construct deeper groundwater wells. A key challenge is that well construction is expensive and a safe, cost effective policy would install a well that reduces nitrate concentrations below the regulatory limit while minimizing the depth of the well.
A pragmatic policy to mitigate harmful contaminants in drinking water requires policies that not only satisfy regulatory standards, but also minimize implementation costs. One prominent example is nitrate, a common groundwater contaminant often linked to agricultural activities. A common strategy for reducing nitrate exposure in drinking water is to construct deeper groundwater wells. However, because well construction is costly, a safe, cost-effective policy should identify the shallowest well depth that reduces nitrate concentrations below the regulatory threshold. Motivated by this challenge, this paper proposes a statistical framework for learning safe and cost-effective mitigation policies for environmental contaminants. At each spatial location, the proposed policy selects the minimum intervention level (e.g., well depth) to achieve a prespecified contaminant threshold (e.g., nitrate concentrations below regulatory standard). Two central features of our framework are (1) the explicit treatment of spatial confounding and spatial dependence, which is unavoidable in this setting, and (2) empirical risk minimization estimation using a novel, nonparametric, doubly robust loss function. We derive excess-risk bounds for the proposed estimator and formally quantify the impact of spatial dependence. We conclude by applying our method to design a safe, cost-effective nitrate-mitigation policy for Wisconsin.
\end{abstract}


\section{Introduction}


\subsection{Motivation: Nitrate Mitigation in Drinking Water}

For more than two decades, nitrate $(\mathrm{NO_3^{-}})$ has been among the three most common contaminants in drinking water supplies \citep{Pennino2017}. Excessive nitrate in groundwater is primarily caused by human activities such as fertilizer applications, manure spreading, or leaky shallow septic systems. Elevated nitrate levels have been linked to certain cancers and adverse fetal outcomes during pregnancy \citep{Ward2005}. As a result, the United States Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) has set a maximum contaminant level (MCL) of 10 mg/L that is enforceable in all public water systems \citep{USEPA1995a}.

% Excess nitrate in drinking water typically occur in areas with intense agricultural activities \citep{Sobota2013, Sabo2018}, well-drained soils, and shallow water supply wells \citep{Glenn2010,Enwright2009}.
% Formed when nitrogen from fertilizers or other sources oxidizes, nitrate is highly water-soluble and does not adsorb well to soil particles. Consequently, it is easily transported into groundwater by precipitation and melting snow percolating through soil and bedrock.

 A common and effective intervention for reducing nitrate exposure in drinking water sourced from groundwater aquifers is to increase the depth of the wells so that the wells draw water deeper within the aquifer system (e.g., \citet{Spalding1993, nitrate2025}).
However, installing deep wells is expensive, especially for private well owners who are not connected to public water systems \citep{nitrate2025}. Instead, a cost-effective approach is a ``personalized,'' location-specific solution where drillers are advised to install wells to a minimum depth necessary to remain below the MCL.

More broadly, the paper is motivated by a wide array of policies in groundwater contaminants, along with air, surface water, and ecological regulations that must achieve some optimal solution under \textit{ex ante} uncertainty. 
The paper is especially relevant to non-point source groundwater contaminants such as pesticides \citep{Stackelberg2012}, per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) \citep{park2024statistical, Guo2022, Gnesda2022}, and excessive chloride in groundwater systems \citep{Pieper2018}. Similarly, many contaminants in surface water are closely monitored, and mitigation of surface water contaminants such as nitrate and phosphorus requires the implementation of nutrient best management practices \citep{Dai2018}. Policies such as buffer strips, cover crops, grade stabilization, or detention wetlands may be implemented and optimized to meet downstream nutrient thresholds 
%Like contaminants in water, exceedances of air quality thresholds may require siting or upgrading air pollution control equipment when local atmospheric conditions make it uncertain whether standards will be met after installation. For example, \citet{esworthy2013air} and \citet{papadogeorgou2019causal}discuss interventions to control particulate matter in the air (PM 2.5) while \citet{goin2021hyperlocalized} measures the effect of potential interventions on nitrogen dioxide, black carbon, and ultra-fine particulates.  
and the paper's cost-effective policy provides one solution to design interventions that balance policy benefits and costs.


% We briefly remark that while this study is motivated by nitrate mitigation policies in drinking water wells, the proposed statistical framework could be applicable to a wide array of groundwater contaminants, along with air, surface water, and ecological regulations that must achieve some optimal solution under \textit{ex ante} uncertainty. Our approach is especially relevant to non-point source groundwater contaminants such as pesticides \citep{Stackelberg2012}, per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) \citep{park2024statistical, Guo2022, Gnesda2022}, and excessive chloride in groundwater systems \citep{Pieper2018}. Similarly, many contaminants in surface water are closely monitored, and mitigation of surface water contaminants such as nitrate and phosphorus requires the implementation of nutrient best management practices \citep{Dai2018}. Policies such as buffer strips, cover crops, grade stabilization, or detention wetlands may be implemented and optimized to meet downstream nutrient thresholds 
%Like contaminants in water, exceedances of air quality thresholds may require siting or upgrading air pollution control equipment when local atmospheric conditions make it uncertain whether standards will be met after installation. For example, \citet{esworthy2013air} and \citet{papadogeorgou2019causal}discuss interventions to control particulate matter in the air (PM 2.5) while \citet{goin2021hyperlocalized} measures the effect of potential interventions on nitrogen dioxide, black carbon, and ultra-fine particulates.

\subsection{Our Contributions}

%Inspired by the nitrate mitigation challenge in drinking water,
This paper proposes a spatial policy learning framework to learn safe, cost-effective interventions for contaminant mitigation in the environment. Specifically, among all policy functions $\theta$, which maps the observed characteristics $X$ at spatial location $S$ (e.g., fertilizer applications, land use at location $S$) to an intervention level $a$ (e.g., well depth), we want to find the policy function $\theta^*$ that minimizes the intensity of the intervention while ensuring the outcome remains below a safety threshold $\mathcal{T}$ (e.g., nitrate concentration is below 10mg/L). This can be loosely stated as the following constrained, optimization problem
\begin{equation}\label{eq:brief}
    \theta^*(X,S)=\arg \min_{\theta\in\Theta} \theta(X,S) \quad\text{such that}\quad \mathcal{Y}(\theta(X,S))\leq \mathcal{T}. %\quad\text{for all}\quad i = 1,\ldots,n,
\end{equation}
\noindent The set $\Theta$ is the set of all policy functions, including a policy where intervention level is set to the highest possible value regardless of location (e.g., drill all wells to the maximum aquifer depth) or a policy where the intervention level increases as the distance to known sources of contaminants decreases (e.g. drill a well to depth $a$ where $a$ increases as $S$ gets closer to farms with manure spreading). The term $\mathcal{Y}(\theta(X,S))$ is the expected potential outcome (e.g., nitrate level) at location $S$ under intervention level specified by policy $\theta(X,S)$; see Section \ref{sec:notation} for definition of potential outcomes. We refer to $\theta^* \in \Theta$ as an optimal policy in that it suggests the minimum intervention level necessary to achieve the safety compliance defined by threshold $\mathcal{T}$; in short, $\theta^*$ is safe and cost-effective.



A hallmark of our framework is the causal identification of $\theta^*$ accounts for \emph{unmeasured} spatial characteristics at location $S$ as long as the unmeasured spatial characteristics are ``spatially smooth'' and ``sufficient'' with respect to $S$ (see Section \ref{sec:identify_spatial_confounding}).
% A hallmark of our framework is that we allow $\theta^*$ to account for both \emph{measured} and \emph{unmeasured} spatial characteristics at location $S$ so long as the unmeasured spatial characteristics are ``spatially smooth'' and ``sufficient'' with respect to $S$ (see Section \ref{sec:identify_spatial_confounding}).
This feature is important because in practice, we cannot measure every characteristic at location $S$ that affects the outcome and/or the intervention, a phenomenon broadly referred to as \emph{spatial confounding} (see Section \ref{sec:prior_works}). For example, in the nitrate mitigation example, it is impossible to characterize all hydrogeological features that govern subsurface transport of nitrate.


Also, a remarkable statistical feature of our framework is that the estimation of the policy function $\theta^*$ from $n$ spatially dependent samples can be re-casted as a familiar problem in empirical risk minimization (ERM):
% An important statistical feature of our framework is that nonparametric (or parametric) estimation of the policy function $\theta^*$ from $n$ spatially dependent samples can be recast as a familiar problem in empirical risk minimization (ERM). For example, in the case where the investigator desires a simple, interpretable parametric policy (FIX TO BE GENERAL) function, the corresponding ERM would be
\[
\hat{\theta} = \underset{\theta  \in\Theta}{\mathrm{argmin}} \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^{n} L^{(\delta_n)}(\theta,O_i) + \mathcal{P}_n(\theta).
\]
Here, $\Theta$ can either be a parametric function class for interpretability, or a nonparametric function class for flexibility. The loss function $L^{(\delta_n)}$ is new in that the resulting $\hat{\theta}$ has (i) double robustness to model mis-specification, (ii) improve rates of convergence by kernelized interpolation, and (iii) adaptivity to underlying spatial dependence (see next paragraph). We refer to the loss function as \emph{kernelized doubly robust loss function} and Section \ref{sec:estimator} contains more details. Finally, $\mathcal{P}_n$ is an $L_2$-like penalty term for the complexity of $\theta$.

%we have developed it to simultaneously account for (i) model mis-specification, especially for the outcome regression function (e.g., the model for nitrate transport as a function of spatial covariates and depth), (ii) improved rates of convergence by a $\delta_n$-``kernelized'' interpolation of the outcomes by similar intervention levels, and (iii) adaptivity to spatial dependence. We refer to the loss function as \emph{kernelized doubly robust loss function} and Section \ref{sec:estimator} contains more details.  Finally, $\mathcal{P}_n$ is an $L_2$-like penalty term for the complexity of $\theta$.


% \CP{1. In Section 1.2, we highlighted identification and estimation of $\theta$, so we may also want to mention identification? \textcolor{blue}{from Xindi:see above}\\ 2. 
 %This ERM also applies to nonparametric policy, so maybe write more generally (I think we can just mention $\Theta$ is a policy class, which can be either parametric or nonparametric \textcolor{blue}{from Xindi:see above}\\
 %3. Are the properties (i) and (ii) are relevant to $\hat{\theta}$? Then I think it may be better to write ``This loss function is special in that the resulting $\hat{\theta}$ has robustness and improved rate''} 

Theoretically, %Despite its simplicity, a precise, theoretical analysis of ERM is challenging under spatially dependent training data. Our theoretical analysis 
we explicitly quantify the effect of spatial dependence on the statistical properties of $\hat{\theta}$ from ERM; to the best of our knowledge, this type of theoretical analysis is uncommon in ERMs and may be of independent interest for both causal and non-causal works on nonparametric function estimation under spatial dependence. Notably, while our ERM is designed for spatially dependent observations via the new loss function $L^{(\delta_n)}$, our theoretical results and methodology naturally recover the ``standard'' ERM properties established under independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) setting if the spatial dependence approaches zero. In other words, the loss function can automatically adjust for presence (or absence) of spatial dependence and we refer to this property as adaptivity to spatial dependence.
%\CP{It may be helpful to frame this as a generalization of ERM: as the spatial dependency approaches zero, the formulation reduces to standard ERM (i.e., adaptivity).}\XL{see above}

We briefly remark that while the paper focuses on continuous interventions since it is the most difficult setting from a nonparametric, statistical estimation perspective (see Section \ref{sec:direct_method}), our framework can be simplified to cover non-binary, discrete interventions where larger values of the intervention correspond to increased costs (see Section \ref{sec: discussion}). 
%\CP{Maybe include social science examples, e.g., location-based tax policies?}\XL{see above}

\subsection{Prior Works} \label{sec:prior_works}

% \textcolor{red}{to chan: double-check whether we missed any important stat references that you know}

Our paper is most closely related to existing works in causal inference on  single-time, dynamic policies under constraints 
\citep{Laber2018, Ren2022,jia2025bayesian,gu2025safe, wu2021offline,qiu2022individualized,viviano2020experimental, ma2021causal,sun2024empirical}. Distinct from these works , \citet{KitagawaTetenov2018, athey2021policy,Kitagawa2021} enforced interpretability and resource constraints by restricting the class of feasible policies. 
% \CP{Need to add \url{https://dviviano.github.io/projects/experimental_design_2020.pdf}, \url{https://proceedings.mlr.press/v130/ma21c/ma21c.pdf}, \url{https://lsun20.github.io/EWM_constraints.pdf} and references therein} \XL{Done}
Also, our optimization problem in \eqref{eq:brief} is similar to those in \citet{chen2022policy}, \citet{park2023personalized}, and \citet{park2024minimum}, which studied policy learning under similar resource constraints. But, the main difference between the aforementioned works and ours is that we deal with spatial dependence due to spatial confounding (see below), which leads to dramatically different identifying assumptions, estimators, and statistical properties. \textcolor{red}{to CP and XL: check? For instance, one major difference is the loss functions where the loss function in this paper leads to rate-improving interpolation and spatial adaptivity.} For clarity, we highlight specific differences between the existing works and our work throughout the manuscript.

Our work broadly falls under shape-constrained,  nonparametric regression under spatial dependence, specifically dependence due to \emph{spatial confounding} \citep{paciorek2010importance,hodges2010adding,zigler2021spatial,reich2021review}. Spatial confounding is the presence of spatially correlated unmeasured confounders across study units and differs from ``traditional'' unmeasured confounders where the unmeasured confounders are independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) across units. Because of the dependence in the unmeasured confounders, the outcomes or the interventions between study units exhibit dependence. Consequently, different assumptions are necessary to  identify and estimate intervention effects under spatial confounding (e.g., \citet{thaden2018structural,papadogeorgou2019adjusting,khan2022restricted,gilbert2021causal,dupont2022spatial+,wiecha2024two,gilbert2024consistency,datta2025consistent,burman2025robust}). To the best of our knowledge, existing works on spatial confounding focus on identification and estimation of \emph{low-dimensional parameters} (e.g., average treatment effects in \citet{gilbert2024consistency}). In contrast, our work focuses on identification and estimation of \emph{nonparametric functions} (e.g., optimal policies) under spatial confounding, especially if they are under some constraints imposed by the policymaker. %, which are essentially nonparametric functions with certain shape constraints.

We briefly remark that there is growing work on geospatial machine learning  
\citep{sigrist2022gaussian,saha2023random,zhan2024neural} where the goal is to estimate nonparametric prediction models that account for spatial dependence. While our work focuses on measuring causal effects of interventions rather than generating predictions, we borrow some techniques from these works to flexibly estimate nuisance parameters in the optimal policy function (see Section \ref{sec:nuisance}). %and (b) characterize the rates of convergence of under spatial dependence.

%\citet{gilbert2021causal} extend the double machine learning framework \citep{colangelo2025double, chernozhukov2018double} to spatially dependent data under weak dependence. 

%gradient boosting \citep{sigrist2022gaussian}, random forests \citep{saha2023random}, and neural networks  \citep{zhan2024neural}.

%In spatial statistics, a popular way to estimate high-dimensional functionals under spatial dependence in the observed data is with Gaussian processes (GPs); see \citet{cressie2015statistics} for a textbook introduction. In particular, in geospatial machine learning, this often takes the form of a nonparametric mixed-effects model, $Y(S) = f(X(S)) + U(S) + \epsilon(S)$, where $m(\cdot)$ represents the target function of interest and $U(S)$ is a Gaussian process, often coupling these with kriging \citep{cressie2015statistics} to estimate the GP component $U(S)$. 

Our work differs from an important line of work on \emph{spatial interference}, where the counterfactual outcomes of study units are affected by the interventions of their spatial neighbors \citep{verbitsky2012causal,papadogeorgou2019causal,papadogeorgou2023spatial,interfereluo2024policy,zigler2021bipartite, giffin2023generalized}. 
 %or more generally a large volume of works on interference.
 %(e.g., \citet{halloran1991study,sobel2006what, hudgens2008toward, Rosenbaum2007,tchetgen2012causal,liu2020large,aronow2017estimating, Savje2017,forastiere2021identification,li2022random,zhang2023individualized,ogburn2024causal,kandiros2024conflict, %halloran2025designs,hettinger2025doubly,lee2025efficient}) \textcolor{red}{to xindi and chan: should we add more ref here on interference to get better referee feedback? Or should we trim this list?}. 
% \CP{I think these references are overly extensive. In fact, our current setting corresponds to a special case of covariate interference (i.e. neighboring spatial structure may be associated with others' (A,Y)), but there is no treatment interference (i.e. A do not affect others’ Y). so I suggest removing the treatment interference literature; if we were to include additional references, then we may cite work on covariate interference and try to find connections, for example: \url{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29921160/} and \url{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5055852/}
% } \XL{These two references are added. I also think the references of interference are extensive. I guess maybe we could trim the list shorter.}
While there are interventions  that can exhibit spatial interference, this paper's focus is on identifying and estimating policies under spatial dependence due to spatial confounding. 
%We remark that if there are no unmeasured confounders that induce spatial dependence, our problem is an instance of covariate interference \citep{prague2016accounting, Balzer2019} where the outcome of an study unit is affected by the measured covariates of neighboring study units.
Also, in our leading example on  nitrate mitigation in groundwater wells, spatial interference is unlikely as there is no plausible hydrogeological mechanism in which well depth at one location (i.e., the intervention) affects the concentration of a contaminant (i.e., the outcome) at another location. 

%Finally, our work differs from spatial point processes for causal inference \citep{papadogeorgou2022causal,zhou2024estimating,mukaigawara2025spatiotemporal,lin2024efficient}. These models are useful if the goal is to study intervention effects on the distribution of spatial events. In contrast, our work deals with intervention effects where the spatial locations are determined a priori.

Second, we propose an estimator of $\theta^*$ under spatial confounding using ERM with a novel kernelized doubly robust loss function $L^{(\delta_n)}$. %The proposed estimator does not make parametric assumptions about the data generating process and incorporates spatially adjusted kernels to deal with a challenging statistical problem associated with estimating the effect of a continuous intervention under spatial dependence (see Section \ref{sec:estimator}).

Third, we present a new theoretical analysis that explicitly quantifies the impact of spatial dependence on  estimating the policy function $\theta^*$. To the best of our knowledge, excess risk bounds under spatial dependence are largely unavailable, whereas our results explicitly decompose the error into components attributable to spatial dependence and to the smoothness of the class of policy functions.

%we depart from both \citet{gilbert2021causal} and \citet{park2024minimum}

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Section \ref{sec:setup} discusses identification of $\theta^*$ in the presence of spatial confounding. Section \ref{sec:estimator} discusses estimation of $\theta^*$ based on an empirical
risk minimization with a doubly robust loss function and characterizes the excess risk bound of the estimator. Section \ref{sec:other_results} discusses other useful results, including cross fitting, policy learning where the class of policy functions is restricted to be parametric, and fast computational approximation via Vecchia approximation. Section \ref{sec:simulation} applies the proposed method to simulated data and Section \ref{sec:application} uses the proposed method to design a safe, cost-effective nitrate mitigation policy for groundwater wells in Wisconsin.



\section{Causal Identification}\label{sec:setup}

\subsection{Notation and Problem Statement} \label{sec:notation}
 %To measure effects of interventions under spatial confounding, we adopt the potential outcomes framework. 
 Let $\mathcal{S} = \{S_1,S_2,\ldots,S_n\}$ represent the set of $n$ distinct spatial locations within a spatial domain $\mathcal{D} \subset \mathbb{R}^2$. For each location $S_i\in\mathcal{S}$, let $Y_i\in\mathbb{R}$ 
 % \CP{range of $Y$?} \XL{The literature of SVM usually assumes $Y$ be bounded, or could be clipped. However, we consider GP-distributed random effect so the outcome $Y$ is unbounded. In the proof, we overcome it by the concentration inequality of Gaussian distributed random variable}
 be the observed outcome, $A_i\in \mathcal{A}=[a_L,a_U]\subset\R$ be the observed intervention/treatment level within the range $a_L$ and $a_U$,  $X_i\in\mathcal{X}\subset\R^{d+1}$ be the observed measured confounders,  
 % \textcolor{red}{to xindi: check assumption on $X_i$ to match that of spatial random forest paper} \XL{Similar to the SVM literature, we assume the covariate $X$ to be bounded.}
and $U_i\in\R$ be the unmeasured,  spatially-varying confounders. Let $Y_i(a)\in\mathbb{R}$ 
 % \CP{range of $Y(a)$?}  
 be the potential outcome if, contrary to fact, the intervention is set to level $a \in \mathcal{A}$. We use $\mathcal{T} \in \mathbb{R}$ to denote the maximum value of the outcome in order to be compliant with some regulatory standard. 
 
 % \textcolor{red}{to xindi: we should be careful about how we define $S$'s relation to $Y$, $A$, $X$. For example, in our risk theory below, I think we are assuming that technically the case that $Y_i = Y_i(S_i)$, $X_i = X_i(S_i)$ right? Because of the notation clash with the outcome $Y_i(S_i)$ and $Y_i(A_i)$, we should use different notation for $S_i$? In general, it maybe helpful to have a separate paragraph explaining this relationship here? \XL{$Y_i$ and $X_i$ are not necessarily functions of $S_i$. Similar to the geospatial machine learning literature, we would use $Y_i,X_i$ to represent the observation but only use $U_i = g(S_i)$ to denote that the unobserved covariate is a function of $S_i$}}
 
 Given any location $S_0\in\mathcal{D}$, let the subscripts in the variables $Y_0, Y_0(a),X_0,U_0$ denote the variables at location $S_0$. We define the optimal policy at $S_0$ as the minimum level of intervention necessary such that the conditional expectation of the potential outcome is no larger than the threshold $\mathcal{T}$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:def_estimand}
    \theta^*(X_0,S_0) = \arg\min_{a\in\mathcal{A}} \ a, \ \text{such that } \E[Y_0(a)| X_0, U_0]\leq \mathcal{T}.
\end{equation}
%\CP{Maybe this is the place to discuss the monotonicity assumption?} \XL{I put the statement and discussion of monotonicity in Section \ref{sec:estimator}.}
\noindent Our goal is to nonparametrically identify and estimate the policy function $\theta^*$ above with the observed data $O_i = (Y_i, A_i,X_i, S_i)$. Specifically, we first need to write the conditional expectation of the potential outcomes $\E[Y_0(a)| X_0, U_0]$ as a function of the observed data; this exercise is often referred to as causal identification. Second, once \eqref{eq:def_estimand} is written in terms of observed data, we need a robust, nonparametric estimator of $\theta^*$ with good statistical properties.  We briefly remark that unlike \citet{park2024minimum}, (a) the constraint in \eqref{eq:def_estimand} depends on unmeasured variables $U_0$ and (b) the intervention is continuous, but non-interfering. 

In the nitrate example, the set $\mathcal{S}$ is the  locations of groundwater wells. For each well at location $S_i$, $Y_i$ is the observed measured nitrate level at the current well depth $A_i$, $Y_i(a)$ is the nitrate level if the well were to have depth $a$ (not necessarily equal to $A_i$), $X_i$ are measured confounders at $S_i$,  $U_i$ is the unmeasured, spatially varying trend of nitrate that cannot be captured by $X_i$, and $\mathcal{T}$ is the nitrate enforcement standard set by the EPA. The optimal policy $\theta^*(X_0, S_0)$ is the minimum well depth needed at location $S_0$ with measured covariates $X_0$ so that the expected nitrate level is at or below the enforcement threshold $\mathcal{T}$.

\subsection{Causal Identification Under Spatial Confounding} \label{sec:identify_spatial_confounding}
%Let $\Theta = \big\{ \theta \big| \theta(S_0) \in [a_L,a_U], \ S_0 \in \mathcal{D} \}$ be a collection of all functions that map locations in $\mathcal{D}$ to the range of the intervention $[a_l, a_u]$. Specifically, each function $\theta \in \Theta$ is one possible policy function that outputs the intensity of the intervention (e.g., well depth) given the location $S_0$ as an input. 
To identify the optimal policy $\theta^*$ defined in \eqref{eq:def_estimand}, we start with the ``standard'' identifying assumptions in causal inference under spatial confounding \citep{papadogeorgou2019adjusting, schnell2020mitigating, gilbert2021causal}. %Here, identification refers to causal identification where we write the target etimand $\theta^*$ as a function of the observed data $(Y, A, X, S)$.
 \begin{enumerate}[label=(A\arabic*)]
     \item \emph{Causal consistency}:  $Y_i = Y_i(A_i)$ almost surely. \label{condition:consistency}
        \item 
        % \CP{Latent?} \XL{Sounds good!} 
        \emph{Latent Ignorability}: $Y_i(a) \perp A_i \mid X_i, U_i .$ \label{condition:ignorability}
        \item \emph{Overlap}: $p(A_i = a \mid X_i=x, U_i=u) > 0 \text{ for all $a$, $x$, $u$}$ and $p$ is the conditional density function. \label{condition:overlap} 
 \end{enumerate}
% \textcolor{red}{to chan and xindi: should we add a remark about overlap for continuous variables being difficult and add citations on incremental effects?} \CP{I believe this issue is beyond the scope of the paper, particularly because we do not have a well-defined estimand corresponding to $\theta$ when using the incremental propensity score. Considering the incremental effects is easier because it is a population-level quantity (rather than being $X$-specific), whereas $\theta$ is defined at the $X$-specific level. Consequently, it is unclear how we could effectively address the positivity issue.} \XL{I am not sure about discussing positivity. It looks very curcial in application. But I didn't see many discussions about it in the papers that I have read.} 
If $U_i$ is actually observed, Assumptions \ref{condition:consistency} - \ref{condition:overlap} are sufficient to identify the conditional expectation in the constraint of
\eqref{eq:def_estimand}:
%Assumptions \ref{condition:consistency} - \ref{condition:overlap} are extensions of the usual causal identifying assumptions from the classic i.i.d. setting to spatial confounding  (e.g., \cite{papadogeorgou2019adjusting, schnell2020mitigating, gilbert2021causal}). 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:identification_1}
    \E[Y_0(a)| X_0 = x_0,U_0 = u_0] = \E[Y_0| A_0 = a, X_0 = x_0,U_0 = u_0]. 
\end{equation}
\noindent 
% \CP{I'm a bit confused: ``In the presence of spatial confounding'' I thought that even if $U$ is measured, it would still be considered a confounder, and spatial confounding would still exist. My confusion may stem from not fully following the literature, but when we refer to spatial confounding, is the term specifically used to describe situations where $U$ is unmeasured?} \XL{Spatial confounding is a special lucky case of unmeasured confounding. In means that $U$ is spatially varying so we can leverage the location $S$ to mitigate the confounding bias.}
But, in the presence of spatial confounding where $U_i$ is unmeasured, we need more assumptions to identify $ \E[Y_0(a)| X_0 = x_0,U_0 = u_0]$ on the left-hand-side of \eqref{eq:identification_1}. One set of assumptions is to let the locations $S_i$ serve as a proxy for $U_i$ \citep{gilbert2021causal,wiecha2024two, gilbert2024consistency}: 
% \textcolor{red}{to xindi: are there more proper names for these two conditions from prior works? I've simply named them based on their descriptions} \XL{\cite{gilbert2021causal} does not name these conditions. The current names look good to me.}

%Fortunately, when $U_i$ varies spatially with the observed location $S_i$, using $S_i$ as a proxy may mitigate the confounding issue, which is strategy taken by nearly all methods for spatial confounding in the literature, see \citet{gilbert2021causal,wiecha2024two, gilbert2024consistency}. To this end, 
%Formally, we assume two additional assumption under which we can replace $U_i$ in the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:identification_1} by $S_i$, 
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(A\arabic*), start=4]
\item \emph{Spatially smooth confounding}: $U_i = g(X_i,S_i)$ for an unknown, non-random function $g$ that is almost surely continuous. \label{condition:smooth_spatial_confounder} 
\item \emph{Spatial ignorability}: $Y_i(a)\perp A_i\mid X_i, U_i,S_i$.\label{condition:no_confouning_spatial_confounder_weak}
\item \XL{\emph{Spatial sufficiency}: $A_i\perp S_i\mid X_i,U_i$.} \label{condition:spatial_sufficiency}
 \end{enumerate}
Assumption \ref{condition:smooth_spatial_confounder} requires that the unmeasured confounders are functions of the spatial location, and is almost surely continuous. We remark that existing geospatial machine learning prediction models implicitly or explicitly make this assumption, especially when these prediction models are based on nonparametric mixed effect models \citep{sigrist2022gaussian,saha2023random,zhan2024neural}. Assumption \ref{condition:no_confouning_spatial_confounder_weak} builds upon Assumption \ref{condition:ignorability}. It implies that, once we have conditioned on the latent factors $U_i$, the spatial location $S_i$ does not introduce further confounding. \XL{Assumption \ref{condition:spatial_sufficiency} means $S_i$ is only associated with $A_i$ through $X_i,U_i$}; see Figure \ref{fig:causal_DAG} for a visual illustration. 


 % Assumption \ref{condition:no_confouning_spatial_confounder_weak} requires that the intervention is no longer confounded after conditioning on location $S_i$, unmeasured confounders $U_i$, and measured confounders $X_i$. ; see Figure \ref{fig:causal_DAG} for a visual illustration.

%\CP{After reading Gilbert et al.'s paper, I believe I now understand why the assumptions are stated in this particular order. However, I think Assumption (A5) should be interpreted with respect to (A2); that is, assuming (A5) on top of (A2) implies that, conditional on $U$, additionally conditioning on $S$ does not introduce confounding.} \XL{Done}

 \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.9\linewidth]{Causal_DAG_updated.png}
    \caption{
    % \CP{Graphs; (d) and (f) are cyclic}
    Causal directed acyclic graphs illustrating different types of spatial confounding. The measured confounder $X$ is omitted for clarity. Panels (a)–(c) show scenarios where Assumption \ref{condition:no_confouning_spatial_confounder_weak} is satisfied \XL{while Assumption \ref{condition:spatial_sufficiency} is violated in (b)}. Panel (d) is compatible with Assumption \ref{condition:no_confouning_spatial_confounder_weak} but violates Assumption \ref{condition:ignorability}.}
    % while panels (d)–(f) show scenarios where the assumptions are violated. 
    % \CP{(e) is compatible with (A5) but violates (A2), so the description is misleading}
    \label{fig:causal_DAG}
\end{figure}

In the nitrate example, Assumption \ref{condition:smooth_spatial_confounder} is plausible if the unmeasured confounders $U$ are (latent) variables related to subsurface hydrogeological characteristics, such as groundwater recharge and depth of water table. These characteristics can often be smoothly approximated by well-established models for subsurface hydrological transport based on spatially smooth partial differential equations. But, Assumption \ref{condition:smooth_spatial_confounder} may be implausible if $U$ are variables that are not determined only by spatial locations. For example, intensity of agricultural activity may depend on both spatial location $S$ and hidden factors related to economic conditions and infrastructure. 
\CP{This discussion raises the question of whether we could allow $U = g(S,X)$. If so, we can argue that when $X$ contains rich information beyond spatial location, Assumption 4 may be more plausible. If this relaxation is not easy, we can stick to the current setup and discussion.} \XL{Revised. It can be relaxed. But it turned out that Assumption \ref{condition:spatial_sufficiency} would be required for positivity conditioning on $S_i$.} \textcolor{red}{to myself: add paragraph about (A6)'s plausibility and get it checked with Chris.}
Overall, we believe that the most plausible $U$ in our dataset is unmeasured, subsurface hydrogeological characteristics due to the physical processes governing nitrate transport, which influences $Y$, and water levels, which influences $A$. But, our belief is based on discussions with subject-matter experts in hydrogeology and for other datasets, we urge careful discussion of this assumption with domain experts. Finally, even if Assumption \ref{condition:smooth_spatial_confounder} fails to hold, the estimated policy function may still be useful;  see discussion following Proposition \ref{thm:identification}.

For Assumption \ref{condition:no_confouning_spatial_confounder_weak}, we believe it is likely satisfied in the nitrate example by a hydrogeological mechanism described in Figure \ref{fig:causal_DAG}(a). That is, after conditioning on all unmeasured confounders (e.g., hydrogeological features, agricultural activity), location is unlikely to have a direct effect on well depth or nitrate concentrations. We remark that  Figure \ref{fig:causal_DAG}(a) is drawn to  illustrate the assumption $(Y_i(a), A_i) \perp S_i\mid X_i, U_i$, which implies Assumption \ref{condition:no_confouning_spatial_confounder_weak}. 

%We formalize this
%   \begin{enumerate}[label=(A\arabic*.a), start=5]
%\item $ (Y_i(a), A_i)\perp S_i\mid X_i, U_i$. \label{condition:no_confouning_spatial_confounder_strong}
% \end{enumerate} 
%Note that Assumption \ref{condition:no_confouning_spatial_confounder_strong} implies Assumption \ref{condition:no_confouning_spatial_confounder_weak}.

The following proposition shows that under Assumptions \ref{condition:consistency}- \ref{condition:no_confouning_spatial_confounder_weak}, we can identify the optimal policy $\theta^*$ under spatial confounding with the observed data.
%In short, for the nitrate example, the most problematic assumption that requires further analysis in the future is Assumption \ref{condition:smooth_spatial_confounder}.
%solely by spatial coordinates (e.g., well usage, where wells at the same location serve different purposes) or if $U$ is spatially discontinuous (e.g., categorical variables like land use). 
% Therefore, to identify the causal effect of well depth, we must observe all confounders that are either not strictly functions of space or are spatially discontinuous."
% On the other hand, Assumption \ref{condition:smooth_spatial_confounder} can fail to satisfied in two ways. First, $U$ may not be function of space alone. For example, considering well usage as a potential confounder. Wells  at the same location might have different well usage (e.g., private potable wells versus public municipal wells), meaning $U$ is not determined solely by its spatial coordinates. Second, even if $U$ is a function of $S$, it is not necessarily a continuous function of $S$. For example considering land use as a potential unmeasured confounders. While land use is uniquely determined by the spatial coordinates, it is a categorical variable and inherently discontinuous. 
% Therefore, to identify the causal effect of well depth, we must observe all confounders that are either not solely a function of space or are spatially discontinuous.
%Assumption \ref{condition:no_confouning_spatial_confounder_weak} generally holds when the spatial location $S$ is a parent of the nitrate level $Y$ and well depth $A$ (as illustrated in cases (a), (b), (c), and (e) of Figure \ref{fig:causal_DAG}). Conversely, this assumption is violated if $S$ acts as a collider or a descendant of a collider (cases (d) and (f) in Figure \ref{fig:causal_DAG}). Additionally, while case (e) in Figure \ref{fig:causal_DAG} satisfies Assumption \ref{condition:no_confouning_spatial_confounder_weak}, it violates Assumption \ref{condition:ignorability}.
\begin{theorem}[Identification of $\theta^*$]\label{thm:identification}
    % \textcolor{red}{to xindi: (a) if we plan to use $^*$ notation for true value, we should use it consistently (e.g., propensity score) (b) should we use the subscript $E[Y_i \mid A_i,...]$ to be consistent with above?} 
    % Let $\mu^*(a,x,s):=E[Y_i|A_i=a, X_i = x, S_i = s]$. 
    Given $\mathcal{T}, x_{i}, s_{i}$ and Assumptions \ref{condition:consistency} - \ref{condition:no_confouning_spatial_confounder_weak}, $\theta^*$ can be identified by
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:identification}
            \theta^*(x_i,s_i) = \arg\min_{a\in\mathcal{A}} \ a, \ \text{such that } E[Y_i|A_i=a, X_i = x_i, S_i = s_i]\leq \mathcal{T}.
        \end{equation}
         % \CP{I think this should be moved closer to eq \eqref{eq:def_estimand}, since the discussion in Proposition 1 focuses solely on identification.}
If the set in \eqref{eq:identification} is empty, we let $\theta^*(x_i,s_i)=a_L$. %to minimize the expected average outcome. 
\end{theorem}

\XL{
\begin{proof}
It is sufficient to prove the unconfoundedness Assumption $Y_i(a) \perp A_i \mid (X_i, S_i)$ and the overlap Assumption $p(A_i=a|X_i=x,S_i=s_i)>0$. First, we prove the unconfoundedness. Since $U_i = g(X_i, S_i)$ is a measurable function of $(X_i, S_i)$, the triplet $(X_i, S_i, U_i)$ is a measurable function of $(X_i, S_i)$ as well. Since $(X_i, S_i)$ is trivially a function of $(X_i, S_i, U_i)$, there exists a measurable one-to-one mapping between $(X_i, S_i)$ and $(X_i, S_i, U_i)$; therefore, $(X_i, S_i)$ and $(X_i, S_i, U_i)$ give rise to the same conditional distributions (formally, they generate the same $\sigma$-algebra). Consequently, if $Y_i(a) \perp A_i \mid (X_i, S_i, U_i)$, it also holds that $Y_i(a) \perp A_i \mid (X_i, S_i)$. Then, we prove the positivity. By Assumption \ref{condition:overlap} and \ref{condition:spatial_sufficiency}, $p(A_i=a|X_i=x,U_i=u,S_i=s_i)>0$. Since $(X_i, S_i)$ and $(X_i, S_i, U_i)$ is a one-to-one function of $S_i,X_i$, conditioning on $U_i,X_i,S_i$ is equivalent to conditioning on $(X_i, S_i)$. Therefore, $p(A_i=a|X_i=x,S_i=s_i)>0$. 
\end{proof}}
\noindent If the assumptions in Proposition \ref{thm:identification} are not plausible, the optimization problem in \eqref{eq:identification} may still be useful in practice. Specifically, without Assumptions \ref{condition:consistency} - \ref{condition:no_confouning_spatial_confounder_weak},  $\theta^*$ at location $S_0$ is the smallest level of intervention that is \emph{associated} to have an outcome below $\mathcal{T}$, on average. With Assumptions \ref{condition:consistency} - \ref{condition:no_confouning_spatial_confounder_weak}, $\theta^*$ is the smallest level of intervention that \emph{causes} the counterfactual outcome to be below $\mathcal{T}$, on average; in other words,  \eqref{eq:identification} identifies the 
counterfactual policy originally defined in equation \eqref{eq:def_estimand}.  We remark that the estimator of $\theta^*$ discussed below does not rely on the assumptions in Proposition \ref{thm:identification} except that $\mu^*$ is well-defined in the sense that the conditional expectation exists.

Is it too ``controversial'' to say? $\Rightarrow$} In particular, we only need the conditional expectation $\mu^*$ to be well-defined, which does not strictly need the overlap Assumption \ref{condition:overlap} with respect to the unmeasured confounder $U$.

\section{Spatial Empirical Risk Minimization}\label{sec:estimator}

\subsection{Kernelized Doubly Robust Loss Function} 
\label{sec:direct_method}

%\textcolor{red}{to xindi and chan: current paragraph (similar to MRTP paper) $\Rightarrow$} We first present a naive estimator of $\theta^*$ based on indirectly using a geospatial machine learning model. % for the spatial outcome. Formally, let $\widehat\mu$ be an estimator of $\mu^*$ in Proposition \ref{thm:identification}, for instance from a spatial random forest estimator \citep{saha2023random} in geospatial machine learning. Then, under Assumptions \ref{condition:consistency} - \ref{condition:no_confouning_spatial_confounder_weak}, an estimator of $\theta^*(S_0)$ is obtained by replacing $\mu^*$ with $\widehat\mu$ in \eqref{eq:identification} and doing a grid search over the range of the treatment:
% \begin{equation} \label{eq:indirect_method}
% \widehat{\theta}(S_0)=\inf_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left\{a \mid \widehat\mu(a,X_{0},S_0)\leq \mathcal{T}\right\}.
% \end{equation}
% \noindent
% This approach of estimating $\theta^*$ is sometimes referred to as the ``indirect'' method in optimal policy learning under non-spatial settings \citep{chakraborty2013statistical}; see \citet{Deliu2022} for a recent review. 
% %We again remark that Assumption \ref{condition:monotonicity} is not necessary for the indirect approach. 
% Similar to works from this literature, a major limitation of this indirect approach in \eqref{eq:indirect_method} is its reliance on a correctly specified and well-estimated outcome regression model. In particular, unlike the standard i.i.d. settings, it is substantially more challenging to obtain a consistent nonparametric estimator for $\mu^*$ under spatial confounding; see Section \ref{section:outcome_regression}. %Also, similar to \citet{Zhao2012} observation about the indirect approach under non-spatial i.i.d. setting, our indirect approach may have poor finite-sample properties compared to an approach that directly estimates $\theta^*$. 
% In our simulation study in Section \textcolor{red}{xxx}, we reconfirm this observation under spatial confounding setting and, consequently, we recommend the direct approach for estimating $\theta^*$.

Let $\mu^*(a,x,s):=E[Y_i|A_i=a, X_i = x, S_i = s]$ denote the conditional mean outcome. Given the identification result in Proposition  \ref{thm:identification}, a tempting approach to estimate $\theta^*$ is a ``plug-in'' approach (also referred to as an indirect approach) where for a given location $S_0$, we would
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] obtain predictions from $\widehat{\mu}(a,X_0, S_0)$, which denotes an estimate of $\mu^*$, for different intervention levels, and
\item[(ii)] pick the smallest intervention level $a$ such that the constraint $\widehat{\mu}(a,X_0, S_0) \leq \mathcal{T}$ is satisfied.
\end{enumerate}
But, this approach requires a correct specification of the model $\mu^*$. Also, even if $\mu^*$ is correctly specified and can be consistently estimated, say by using flexible geospatial machine learning models (e.g.,  \citet{nandy2017additive,sigrist2022gaussian,saha2023random,zhan2024neural}),
the convergence rate of the estimator $\widehat{\mu}$ to the true model $\mu^*$ is often very slow due to spatial dependence. For example, the mean squared error of spline-based estimators in nonparametric additive model converges at a rate of $O_p\left\{m_n\log(pm_n)\right\}$ in i.i.d. settings, whereas it converges at a slower rate of $O_p\left\{\sigma_nm_n^2\log(pm_n)\right\}$ under spatial dependence. Here, $p$ is the dimension of the covariates, $m_n$ is the number of spline basis, and $\sigma_n$ is the condition number of the working covariance matrix of the spatially dependent errors \citep{nandy2017additive}. \textcolor{red}{to XL and CP: check?: If the distance between neighboring observations approaches zero, $\sigma_n$ with respect to $n$ and can significantly influence the convergence properties of the estimator.}
% \CP{I think it would we useful to mention $\sigma_n$ can grow wrt $n$ under spatial error?}
% \textcolor{blue}{Response from Xindi: the rate would be multiplied by the covariance matrix when common nonparametric method is applied to geo-referenced data.} \textcolor{red}{to xindi: write out this in a few sentences below or simply summarize rate of convergence using some version of the following sentence: ``Roughly speaking, if $\widehat{\mu} - \mu^* = r_n$ for some sequence of random variables $r_n$ in i.i.d. settings and $\widehat{\mu}$ is a parametric estimator, this same estimator will have a convergence rate of 
% $\widehat{\mu} - \mu^* = r_n \sigma_n$(?) under spatial dependence''}


%\subsection{Outcome Regression Modeling With Nonparametric Spatial Mixed Effect Model}\label{section:outcome_regression}

To reduce the above concern about model mis-specification and slow rates of convergence under spatial dependence, our proposed estimator directly estimates $\theta^*$ by recasting it as a solution to ERM with a novel, spatially smooth doubly robust loss function. %By reframing the original optimizaiton problem as a risk minimization problem, we can leverage a wealth of methods in ERM to estimate $\theta^*$ under a wide variety of policy classes $\Theta$.
Formally, %for $a\in[a_L,a_U]$, 
let $p^*(a|X_i,S_i)$ be the generalized propensity score, i.e., the conditional density of observed treatment level $A_i$ given the measured confounders $X_i$ and spatial coordinates $S_i$. 
Let $K_{\delta_n}(A_i-a):={\delta_n}^{-1}K((A_i-a)/{\delta_n})$ be the scaled-kernel function, where ${\delta_n}>0$ is the bandwidth and $K(\cdot)$ is a bounded differentiable kernel function satisfying $\int_\R K(a)\mathrm{d}a = 1$ and $\int_\R a K(a)\mathrm{d}a = 0$. Common choices of $K(\cdot)$ include the Epanechnikov and Gaussian kernels; in this paper, we adopt the Gaussian kernel. 
% \textcolor{red}{to xindi: (a) what is a scaled-kernel function? I would be specific about the conditions for the kernel function (it doesn't have to be Gaussian) (b) we should be consistent with subscript of ${\delta_n}$ (or super-script for loss function $L^{(\delta_n)}$?} \XL{Done}. 
For a given observation $O_i = (Y_i, A_i, X_i, S_i)$ and intervention level $a$, we define the loss function $L^{({\delta_n})}(a,O_i)$ for the risk minimization problem as follows: 
% \textcolor{red}{to xindi: did we define $\epsilon$ or $C_0$?} \XL{Done}
\CP{Maybe it would be better to standardize the notation for $\delta_n$. We currently use $L^{\delta_n}$ in Section 1, $L^{(\delta_n)}$ here, and $K_{\delta_n}$ for the kernel.} \XL{Now we use $L^{(\delta_n)}$ and $K_{\delta_n}$} \textcolor{red}{to CP and XL: should we unify and only use sub-script (or super-script) for $\delta_n$?}
\begin{subequations}\label{eq:loss_function_complete}
    \begin{equation}
        L^{({\delta_n})}(a,O_i) = \begin{cases}\label{eq:loss_function_real_line}
    \nu^{({\delta_n})}(a_L, O_i)+\epsilon -\epsilon \left\{\log( e + a_L- a)\right\}^{-1}& \text{if } -\infty<a<a_L\\
    \nu^{({\delta_n})}(a, O_i)&\text{if }  a_L\leq a\leq a_U\\
    \nu^{({\delta_n})}(a_U, O_i)+\epsilon -\epsilon \left\{\log( e + a- a_U)\right\}^{-1}&\text{if }  a_U<a< \infty
\end{cases}
    \end{equation}
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:loss_function_bounded}
        \nu^{({\delta_n})}(a, O_i) = \int_{\mathcal{A}} \{ \mathcal{T} - \psi^{({\delta_n})}(a^\prime,O_i) \} 1(a^\prime\leq a)\mathrm{d}a^\prime + C_0,
    \end{equation}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pseudo_outcome}
    \psi^{({\delta_n})}(a,O_i) = \mu^*(a,X_i,S_i)+ \frac{ \{ Y_i - \mu^*(A_i,X_i,S_i) \} K_{\delta_n}(A_i-a)}{p^*(A_{i}|X_{i},S_{i})}.
\end{equation}
\end{subequations}
Here, $\epsilon>0$ is any positive constant, and $C_0$ is any large constant guaranteeing $\nu^{({\delta_n})}(a,O_i)\geq 0$. Within $a \in [a_L,a_U]$, the loss function is equal to $\nu^{({\delta_n})}(a, O_i)$. Outside of $[a_L, a_U]$, the loss function is strictly decreasing over $a \in (-\infty,a_L)$ and increasing over $a \in (a_U,\infty)$; this construction ensures that the minimizer of the loss function does not fall outside of the range $[a_{L}, a_U]$. The function $\nu^{({\delta_n})}(a, O_i)$ in the loss function is the average difference between a pre-specified threshold $\mathcal{T}$ and the pseudo-outcome $\psi^{({\delta_n})}(a,O_i)$, which serves as a more robust substitute for the conditional mean outcome $\mu^*$. Specifically, the pseudo-outcome $\psi^{({\delta_n})}(a,O_i)$ %in \eqref{eq:pseudo_outcome} 
resembles the augmented inverse probability weighting term 
in policy learning for a continuous intervention in i.i.d settings \citep{chen2016personalized, kallus2018policy, chen2022policy, colangelo2025double} where the outcome regression model $\mu(a,X_i,S_i)$ is augmented by a term that takes a smooth average of the inverse probability weights of the observed treatments $A_i$ that are near $a$. 
% \CP{We previously claimed that the loss function is doubly robust, but this claim has not been formally established and should be stated more carefully.} \XL{Is it OK if we state the property in \eqref{lemma:risk_db}}

We now make this robustness property precise as follows. Suppose the following assumption holds.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(B\arabic*),start=1]
     \item \emph{Monotonic Response}: $\mu^*(a,x,s)$ is monotonically decreasing in $a$ for any $x,s$. \label{condition:monotonicity}
\end{enumerate}
Assumption \ref{condition:monotonicity} is based on both theoretical and practical considerations. Theoretically, the monotonicity assumption leads to a unique minimum in the ERM. Without this assumption or similar assumptions (see \citet{ pouget2019testing,aronow2021spillover,yuan2023two,park2024minimum} for examples in policy learning under dependence), the optimal intervention level and the corresponding optimal policy $\theta^*$ may not be unique.  %We also remark that similar monotonicity assumptions are imposed in causal inference in dependence settings \citep{pouget2019testing,aronow2021spillover,yuan2023two,park2024minimum}. 
%\citet{park2024minimum} make the same assumption when studying a version of the problem without spatial confounding and with binary treatment. Similar monotonicity assumption is commonly imposed in causal inference in dependence settings \citep{pouget2019testing,aronow2021spillover,yuan2023two}.
Practically, many interventions for contaminant mitigation including our nitrate example satisfy Assumption \ref{condition:monotonicity} in that the intervention to mitigate the contaminant usually has a monotonic relationship with the level of the contaminant (i.e., the outcome). In Section \ref{sec:other_application} of the supplementary materials, we provide a detailed list of examples where the assumption is plausible. \XL{Also, Assumption \ref{condition:monotonicity} can be assessed by computing the empirical derivative of the estimated nonparametric regression model for $\mu^*$ with respect to $a$. See Section \ref{sec:monotonic_assessment} for details and the empirical assessment of Assumption \ref{condition:monotonicity} in the nitrate dataset/}
\CP{It is important to note that this assumption is testable using the observed data. As a result, it does not need to be justified solely on the basis of subject-matter knowledge; instead, it can be evaluated using the estimated outcome regression. Thus, it would also be helpful to include a diagnostic, similar to the one presented on page 45 of my MRTP paper: \url{https://arxiv.org/pdf/2111.09932}} \XL{Done. See Section \ref{sec:monotonic_assessment}}
% \CP{We may also consider citing \url{https://academic.oup.com/biomet/article/106/4/929/5578431} , \url{https://arxiv.org/abs/2001.05444} , and \url{https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.09790} to note that monotonicity is a commonly imposed assumption in dependence settings}% The monotonicity assumption is inspired by \citet{pouget2019testing,aronow2021spillover,yuan2023two,park2024minimum}, who studied a version of the problem without spatial confounding and with binary treatment. It is also commonly imposed assumption in general dependence settings
%Also, for many interventions for contaminant mitigation including our nitrate example, the monotonicity between the intervention of interest and the level of the contaminant (i.e., the outcome) is plausible; see Section \ref{sec:other_application} of the supplementary materials for details. 
The following lemma shows that under Assumption \ref{condition:monotonicity}, the limiting risk achieves its minimum when $a$ equals $\theta^*$ in \eqref{eq:identification} if either $\mu^*$ or $p^*$ is correctly specified. 
% \textcolor{red}{To xindi: is this the counterfactual estimand or the one based on the solution to the identification problem?} \XL{based on the solution to the identification problem} 

\begin{lemma}[Doubly Robust Risk Minimization Property]\label{lemma:risk_db} 
% \textcolor{red}{to xindi: (a) as discussed above about the non-causal nature of the optimization problem, do we need these assumptions? It's okay if we need these assumptions.} 
% Suppose Assumptions \ref{condition:consistency} - BLANK and $\mu^*(a,x,s)$ is monotonically decreasing in $a$ for any $x$, $s$, and $\mu^*(a_U,x,s)\leq \mathcal{T}$. 
%the following assumption hold:
  %\begin{enumerate}[label=(A\arabic*), start=6]
  %      \item Monotonicity: $\mu^*(a,x,s)$ is monotonically decreasing in $a$ for any $x$, $s$, and $\mu^*(a_U,x,s)\leq \mathcal{T}$. \label{condition:monotonicity}
 %\end{enumerate}
  Let $R(\theta) = \lim_{{\delta_n}\rightarrow 0}\E[L^{({\delta_n})}(\theta,O_i)]$ be the limiting risk function and suppose Assumption \ref{condition:monotonicity} holds. Then, the optimal policy $\theta^*$ defined in 
 % \textcolor{red}{to xindi: double-check this based on comment above} \XL{I change it to the estimand in the identification.} 
 \eqref{eq:identification} is the minimizer of the risk $R(\theta)$, i.e., $R(\theta^*)\leq R(\theta)$ for any $\theta\in\Theta$ where $\Theta$ is the collection of all functions from $\mathcal{X}\times \mathcal{D}$ to $[a_L,a_U]$ if either the outcome regression model $\mu^*$ or generalized propensity $p^*$ is correctly specified.
\end{lemma}
\noindent Beyond double robustness, Lemma \ref{lemma:risk_db} provides a direct approach to estimate $\theta^*$ by minimizing the risk function $R(\theta)$ with respect to the loss function in equation \eqref{eq:loss_function_real_line}.  More broadly, the connection to risk minimization allows investigators to use a variety of methods in ERM to estimate $\theta^*$. In Section \ref{subsec:SVM}, we show how to use support vector machines (SVM), a popular type of estimator based on ERM, to estimate $\theta^*$.

\subsection{Estimating Nuisance Functions Under Spatial Dependence} \label{sec:nuisance}
\subsubsection{Spatial Mixed Effect Model for Conditional Mean of Outcome $\mu^*$}

The loss function $L^{({\delta_n})}(a, O_i)$ depends on two unknown nuisance functions: the outcome regression model $\mu^*(a,X_i,S_i)$ and the generalized propensity score $p^*(A_i|X_i,S_i)$. In practice, we need to replace them by their estimates. In i.i.d. settings, these nuisance functions can be nonparametrically estimated using a wide variety of statistical or machine learning methods and for classical nonparametric methods (e.g., kernel regression of \citet{Nadaraya1964} and \citet{Watson1964}), their rates of convergence are well-established. However, under spatial dependence, there are far fewer methods on nonparametric estimation of these nuisance functions and even fewer works on characterizing their rates of convergence. Our paper addresses both of these issues on nuisance function estimation where this section discusses the methodological aspect of these issues and Section \ref{sec:theory} discusses the theoretical aspect. \CP{This seems fine, though Xindi’s input may be needed for a final judgment} \XL{I think this looks OK to me.}


%\XL{I suggest separating this subsection into two different sections. One section describes the use of}
%\CP{I think we should retain a separate (sub)section to emphasize that this is not a trivial task and to highlight it as one of the contributions of this paper; this is related to my previous comment in ``Our Contributions'' section.}
%\textcolor{red}{to chan and xindi: I'm not sure how to integrate this section into paper? We can either (a) put this in auxiliary results (i.e., makes this section feel more novel/interesting for spatial settings) or (b) integrate it here like the MRTP paper (i.e., reads better for policy learning). It would be great if whatever organization we choose, the organization emphasizes the spatial aspect of the paper and separates itself from MRTP for review purposes.}
%In general, nonparametric estimation of $\mu^*$ \textcolor{blue}{and $p^*$} under spatial dependence/\textcolor{blue}{confounding} must address an overfitting problem that does not exist under i.i.d. settings.

We begin by first highlighting a flaw with a naive methodological solution. Suppose an investigator ignored spatial dependence and  used existing nonparametric methods designed for i.i.d. data, such as kernel regression or random forests \citep{breiman2001random} to estimate $\mu^*$. It is well-known that the estimator will overfit to the spatial coordinate $S_i$ and lead to poor out-of-sample performance in new locations \citep{saha2023random,zhan2024neural}. \XL{No, they usually use basis function, previous paper states that that might suffer from curse of dimensionally and cannot account for the spatial dependence.} %; see \citet{park2024statistical} for one recent example to predict PFAS concentrations across the United States. 
This problem persists even after applying a transformation on $S_i$ to discourage overfitting, such as transforming $S_i$ to be the distance to a universal, baseline location or representing $S_i$ as truncated radial basis functions \citep{hengl2018random,chen2020deepkriging,gray2022use}. Also, under spatial confounding, overfitting to $S_i$ may lead to a violation of Assumption \ref{condition:smooth_spatial_confounder} where the relationship between $U_i$ and $S_i$ is no longer continuous.


%Unlike the standard i.i.d. setting where there are a wealth of methods to estimate $\mu^*$ nonparametrically  %(e.g., \citet{Nadaraya1964, Watson1964, Breiman2001,goodfellow2016deep,hastie2017generalized}), 

To avoid the above issues, we consider a nonparametric spatial mixed effect model, a general framework for predictive modeling in modern geospatial 
statistics \citep{sigrist2022gaussian,saha2023random, zhan2024neural}. Formally, for all $S_i \in \mathcal{D}$, consider the following nonparametric spatial mixed effects model of the outcome:
% \textcolor{red}{to xindi: make sure this equation is correctly stated?} \XL{Done.}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:spatial_mixed_effect_model}
    Y_i = f^*(A_i,X_i) + U(S_i)+\epsilon_i, \qquad U(\cdot)\sim \mathrm{GP}(0,C(\cdot,\cdot|\psi^*)),\quad \epsilon_i\sim N(0,\sigma_\epsilon^{*2}).
\end{equation}
\XL{Yes, normality is required, it is like nugget effect in Gaussian process.}

\CP{A potential quetion from reviewers: Can we reasonably describe our method as ``nonparametric,'' given that we employ a ``semiparametric'' model for $Y$? I wanted to check whether model (6) would typically be regarded as ``nonparametric'' within the spatial statistics community.} \XL{I think so. Because the fixed effect term $f^*$ is nonparametric. $U$ is a random effect. }

% \marginpar{\tiny Chan: Reviewed up to this point, Apr 03, 9am}

% \CP{I prefer to use $^*$ to denote the ``true'' parameters we aim to estimate, but we can follow Xindi’s notation style.}
% \textcolor{red}{to xindi and chan: should we make $f$ as $f^*$? Same for $\psi$ to $\psi^*$} 
The term $f^*(A_i,X_i)$ is a non-random, nonparametric function of the treatment level $A_i$ and the observed covariates $X_i$; note that we allow for non-parametric interactions between $A_i$ and $X_i$. The term $U(\cdot)$ is a mean-zero Gaussian process (GP) with a parametric covariance function $C(\cdot,\cdot|\psi^*)$ parametrized by a low-dimensional parameter $\psi^*$.  Specifically, for $n$ observations of the spatial locations $S_1,\ldots,S_n$, we have $(U(S_1),\ldots,U(S_n))^\top \sim \mathrm{N}(0,\mathbf{C}_n(\psi^*))$ where $\mathbf{C}_n(\psi^*)$ is the $n\times n$ matrix with entries $\mathrm{cov}(U(S_i),U(S_j)) = C(S_i,S_j;\psi^*)$. In this article, we assume $C(S_i,S_j|\psi^*)$ to be Mat\'ern covariance function owing to its remarkable property of characterizing smoothness of Gaussian processes  \citep{stein1999interpolation}. The stationary (isotropic) Matérn covariance function is specified by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:matern}
    C(S_i, S_j|\psi^*) = \sigma_{gp}^{*2}\frac{2^{1-\tau_{gp}^{*}}}{\Gamma(\tau_{gp}^{*})} \left( \sqrt{2\tau_{gp}^{*}} \frac{\|S_i - S_j\|_2}{\gamma_{gp}^{*}} \right)^{\tau_{gp}^{*}} K_\tau \left( \sqrt{2\tau_{gp}^{*}} \frac{\|S_i - S_j\|_2}{\gamma_{gp}^{*}} \right),\quad  \psi^* = (\sigma_{gp}^{*},\gamma_{gp}^{*},\tau_{gp}^{*}),
\end{equation}
where $K_\tau$ is the modified Bessel function of the second kind. 
The Matérn covariance function has three parameters: $\sigma_{gp}^{*}$ controls the variance, $\gamma_{gp}^{*}$ controls the range of the spatial dependence and $\tau_{gp}^{*}$ controls the smoothness (mean-square differentiability) between $U_i$ and $S_i$.
% We remark that the covariance function depends on the spatial locations $S_1,\ldots,S_n$ and the smoothness of this function implicitly controls the smoothness between $U_i$ and $S_i$. 
% Similar to traditional random effects in a linear mixed effects model, the GP-distribution of $U_i$ induces dependence on the outcomes.
% But, unlike linear mixed effects model, the ``random effect'' $U_i$ may be dependent of measured variables $A_i$ and $X_i$ in the model. 


Under the nonparametric mixed effects model in \eqref{eq:spatial_mixed_effect_model}, we propose to estimate $\mu^*$.
\begin{step}[Estimation of $f^*$]\label{step:regression} Apply supervised machine learning method based on generalized least squares (GLS; see below) to obtain an estimator of $f^*$, denoted as $\widehat{f}$.
\end{step}

\begin{step}[Estimation of GP Covariance Function $C(\cdot; \psi^*)$]\label{step:covariance_estimation} 
% \textcolor{red}{to x: should we make this more explicit by writing out the covariance matrix expression, either here or above when we define the nonparametric mixed effects model? Response from Xindi: we can do that, some papers explicitly write the formula of Mat\'ern covariance function. I can cite more more paper.} \XL{Done. Follows}
Let $R_i:=U(S_i)+\epsilon_i$. From \eqref{eq:spatial_mixed_effect_model},  $(R_1,\ldots,R_n)^\top\sim \mathrm{N}(0,\Sigma_n(\psi^*,\sigma_\epsilon^*))$ where $\Sigma_n(\psi^*,\sigma_\epsilon^*) = \mathbf{C}_n(\psi^*)+\sigma_\epsilon^{*2}\mathbf{I}_n$. Let $\widehat R_i=Y_i - \widehat f(A_i,X_i)$ be the estimation of the $R_i$ and $\widehat{\mathbf{R}} := (\widehat R_1,\ldots,\widehat R_n^\top)$. We estimate $(\psi^*,\sigma_\epsilon^{*})$ via maximizing the log-likelihood  of ${\mathbf{R}}$ where ${\mathbf{R}}$ is substituted by their estimates $\widehat{\mathbf{R}}$:
$$(\widehat\psi,\widehat\sigma_\epsilon) = \arg\max_{\psi,\sigma_{\epsilon}}\left\{-\frac{n}{2}\log(|\Sigma_n(\psi,\sigma_\epsilon)|)-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^n \widehat{\mathbf{R}}^\top\Sigma_n^{-1}(\psi,\sigma_\epsilon)\widehat{\mathbf{R}}\right\}.$$
            
\end{step}

\begin{step}[Estimation of $U(\cdot)$]\label{step:kriging}For any location $S_i\in\mathcal{D}$,
% to account for the cases that $S_0\in \mathcal{S}$, i.e., $S_0$ be one of the observed locations, 
 let $\mathcal{S}_{-i} =\mathcal{S}\backslash S_0$ be the set of locations excluding $S_i$ and  $\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{-i}$ be the vector $\widehat{\mathbf{R}}$ excluding  $\widehat R_i$. Also, let $\Sigma_{n,-i}(\widehat\psi,\widehat\sigma_\epsilon)$ be the estimated covariance function of $\mathbf{R}_{-i}$, let $\mathbf{v}_{n,i}^\top(\psi,\sigma_\epsilon)$ be the vector of the estimated covariance between $R_i$ and ${\mathbf{R}}_{-i}$. Then, we estimate $U(S_i)$ via
\begin{equation}\label{eq:kriging}
    \widehat U(S_i) = \mathbf{v}_{n,i}^\top(\widehat\psi,\widehat\sigma_\epsilon)\Sigma^{-1}_{n,-i}(\widehat\psi,\widehat\sigma_\epsilon)\widehat{ \mathbf{R}}_{-i}.
\end{equation}
\end{step}
\begin{step}[Estimation of $\mu^*$]\label{step:or} For any given level of treatment $a\in\mathcal{A}$, the estimated outcome regression is 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:or_estimation}
    \widehat\mu(a,X_i,S_i) = \widehat f(a,X_i)+ \widehat U(S_i).
\end{equation}
\end{step}
%\begin{remark}[Estimating $f^*$ in Step 1 Under Spatial Confounding]
\noindent When estimating $f^*$ in Step \ref{step:regression}, machine learning methods that use (unweighted) squared error loss function (e.g., the loss function for ordinary least squares (OLS)) are known to be inconsistent under spatial confounding \citep{gilbert2024consistency}. Instead, recent works have shown that using a version of GLS yields a consistent estimator of $f$ in parametric \citep{gilbert2024consistency, datta2025consistent} and nonparametric \citep{sigrist2022gaussian,saha2023random, zhan2024neural} settings. Given these existing works, we recommend researchers use some variation of GLS to estimate $f^*$ in Step 1. 
% We briefly remark that in the absence of spatial confounding, but potentially spatial dependence (i.e., when $U_i$ is independent of $A_i$ and $X_i$), both OLS-based and GLS-based machine learning estimators are consistent. However, the GLS-based estimator remains advantageous, as it will be more statistically efficient; see \citet{sigrist2022gaussian,saha2023random, zhan2024neural} for details.
% We also note that when $U_i$ is independent with $A_i,X_i$, i.e., only spatial dependence, no spatial confounding, both OLS-machine learning and GLS-machine learning can obtain consistent estimator of $f(\cdot,\cdot)$ but the GLS-machine learning estimator will be more efficient.
Step \ref{step:covariance_estimation}-\ref{step:kriging} have been used in applications of random forests and neural networks to the nonparametric spatial mixed effect model \eqref{eq:spatial_mixed_effect_model} and has been referred to as \textit{residual kriging} \citep{saha2023random, zhan2024neural}. The estimate $\widehat U(S_i)$ obtained via \eqref{eq:kriging} can be interpreted as the effect of the unmeasured confounder $U_i$ on the observed outcome $Y_i$, assuming that $(A_i,X_i)$ and $U_i$ have an additive effect on $Y_i$. %Overall, the estimator $\hat{\mu}$ in  \eqref{eq:or_estimation} combines the nonparametrically estimated effect of $(A_i,X_i)$ and the GP-driven estimated effect of $U_i$. 

%In Step \ref{step:or}, the outcome regression estimation \eqref{eq:or_estimation} possess the advantage of nonparametrically estimating the mean function of the covariates while retaining the effect of the unmeasusred confounder $U_i$ encoded in the GP covariance function.

\subsubsection{Covariance Balancing Estimator for Conditional Density of Treatment $p^*$}

%\begin{step}[Estimation of $p^*$]\label{step:propensity}
%    We estimate $1/p^*(A_i|X_i,S_i)$ by finding weights that balance the covariates $(X_i,\widehat U(S_i))$ across the observed treatment levels $A_i$. 
%\end{step}

In addition to the outcome regression $\mu^*$, the loss function \eqref{eq:loss_function_complete} requires an estimate $\widehat p(A_i|X_i,S_i)$ of the conditional density $p^*(A_i|X_i,S_i)$. %and it is used to weight each residual using the inverse of the estimated $\widehat p(A_i|X_i,S_i)$ in pseudo outcome \eqref{eq:pseudo_outcome}. 
From above, one approach is to assume a spatial mixed effect model for the observed treatment $A_i$ similar to the outcome regression model in \eqref{eq:spatial_mixed_effect_model}. However, estimating the conditional density requires the correct specification of both conditional mean $\E[A_i|X_i,S_i]$ and the distribution of the residual $A_i - \E[A_i \mid X_i, S_i]$ and the above approach based on nonparametric spatial mixed effects can lead to unstable weights when the distributional assumption is unreasonable. To remedy the problem, we adopt recent covariate balancing methods 
%that directly estimate the weights (instead of the inverse of estimated $\widehat p(A_i|X_i,S_i)$)
that could balance the observed covariate $X_i$ and $\widehat U(S_i)$ obtained via \eqref{eq:kriging} across the range of a continuous treatment 
\citep{fong2018covariate, kallus2019kernel, tubbicke2022entropy, huling2024independence}. For our nitrate example, we use the generalized covariate balancing propensity score of \citet{fong2018covariate}, which is implemented in the R package \texttt{WeightIt} \citep{greifer2025weightit}.


% \begin{remark}[Difference Between Estimating $f^*$ and $\mu^*$ Under Spatial Confounding] In i.i.d. settings, estimating $\mu^*$ and $f^*$ can be treated as equivalent tasks. In other words, steps \ref{step:covariance_estimation} and \ref{step:kriging} are not necessarily and we can simply set $\widehat{\mu} = \widehat{f}$. However, under spatial confounding, the conditional mean of the outcome is not only affected by the observed

% spatial random effect

% we need appropriate way to use location as the proxy of the unmeasured confounders, and model the spatial structure, and spatial correlation of the observations. In this article, we assume an additive effect of $(A_i,X_i)$ and the unmeasured confounder $U_i$, use Gaussian process
