% \XL{actually, I am not sure about it}

% \textcolor{blue}{from xindi: what is the different from traditional method when we account for the spatial/dependence and correlation.}
% \end{remark}



% \begin{remark}[Sample Splitting Under Spatial Dependence and Donsker-Type Conditions]In the standard i.i.d. setting, a popular approach allows the use of modern machine learning to estimate $\mu^*$ and $p^*$ is estimate the nuisance parameters and the target parameter using independent splits of the data \citep{chernozhukov2018double} to avoid the Donsker-Type conditions and present over-fitting bias.

% . \CP{$\Leftarrow$ I think this is only part of the reason. Sample splitting allows us to control the empirical process term even when the Donsker condition does not hold, which we should include. Additionally, although the title of this remark mentions the Donsker condition, we do not actually discuss it, so this also needs to be added.} \XL{Done}

% In spatial data, however, the sample splitting  strategy is complicated by the spatial dependence of the observation. When the observations are identically distributed and the spatial dependence vanish with the distance, the spatial dependence can be overcome by neighbor-excluding cross-fitting, i.e., using subsets that are sufficiently distant to estimate the nuisance parameter and target parameter \citep{gilbert2021causal, semenova2023inference}. Unfortunately, in the nitrate example, the observed nitrate level and covariates are spatially heterogeneous so the assumption of identical distribution does not hold (see Figure \ref{fig:nitrate_visualization}). Thus, we propose to use less sophisticated nonparametric estimator satisfying the Donsker-typen conditions like kernel SVM \citep{christmann2008support} or generalized additive model (GAM) \citep{gam} to estimate $f$ in Step \ref{step:regression}. Additionally, we found that in the nitrate example, the root mean square of the outcome regression estimation \eqref{eq:or_estimation}using kernel SVM in Step \eqref{step:regression} is as good as using random forest in Step \eqref{step:regression}. We think it is because $\widehat U(S_i)$ in \eqref{eq:or_estimation} can compensate the loss of flexibility of $\widehat f(A_i,X_i)$ in \eqref{eq:or_estimation}.Lastly, we want to note that under the identically distributed and weak dependence assumption in \citet{gilbert2021causal,semenova2023inference}, our theory allows the use of modern machine learning method to estimate the nuisance parameter $\mu^*$ and $p^*$. 
% \end{remark}. 

\begin{remark}[Sample Splitting Under Spatial Dependence and Donsker-Type Conditions]
In i.i.d. settings, a common approach to integrate machine learning estimators of $\mu^*$ and $p^*$ is cross-fitting \citep{chernozhukov2018double}. This strategy uses independent subsets to estimate nuisance parameters and the target parameter. Some of the advantages of this strategy include bypassing the need for Donsker-type conditions on the nuisance functions and reducing overfitting bias. 

Unfortunately, in spatial settings, sample splitting is complicated by the inherent dependence between observations. In some settings where the observations are identically distributed and spatial dependence vanishes with distance, this problem
can be addressed through neighbor-excluding cross-fitting, which utilizes subsets of the data that are sufficiently distant from each other to ensure near-independence \citep{gilbert2021causal, semenova2023inference}. In Section \ref{sec:cross-fitting}, we show how to adapt \citet{gilbert2021causal}'s version of sample splitting under spatial dependence to our setting.


In our nitrate example, both the observed nitrate levels and the covariates \XL{violate the identical distribution assumption so neighborhood-excluding sample-splitting used in \citet{gilbert2021causal} fails to produce subsets with the same distribution} (see Figure \ref{fig:nitrate_visualization}). Consequently, our preference for the data analysis is to use nonparametric estimators that satisfy Donsker-type conditions, such as kernel SVM \citep{christmann2008support} or generalized additive models (GAMs) \citep{gam}, to estimate $f$ in Step \ref{step:regression}. Empirically, we observed that in the nitrate example, the root mean square error of the outcome regression \eqref{eq:or_estimation} with an estimate of $f$ from kernel SVM is comparable to that of a random forest. We hypothesize that the inclusion of $\widehat U(S_i)$ in \eqref{eq:or_estimation} effectively compensates for the lower flexibility of $\widehat f(A_i, X_i)$ based on SVM. 
\end{remark}


%For the estimation of the the outcome regression $\mu^*$, as discussed in Section \ref{section:outcome_regression}, we assume a spatial mixed effect model \eqref{eq:spatial_mixed_effect_model} for the outcome regression model and implement Step \ref{step:regression} - Step \ref{step:kriging} to obtain $\widehat\mu$. 
% In Step \ref{step:regression}, we propose to less sophisticated nonparametric estimator like kernel SVM \citep{christmann2008support} or generalized additive model (GAM) \citep{gam} to estimate $f$ for several reasons \textcolor{red}{to xindi: but your theory allows for any model as long as it's trained on a spearate dataset?} \XL{Yes}. First, the use of kernel SVM or generalized additive model allowing us to use the full dataset to estimate the both nuisance parameter and target parameter, avoid assuming assumptions that are implausible and unverifiable. This is because the functional space of kernel SVM and GAM are equipped with an explicit upper bound of its covering number. \CP{I think we also need a phrase clarifying that ``this eventually makes these function classes satisfy the Donsker condition'' as mentioned in the previous remark.} Second, the performance of outcome regression estimation using kernel SVM in our nitrate dataset is as good as using complex machine learning. This is because Step \ref{step:kriging} can compensate for the loss of the flexibility of kernel SVM and generalized additive model. \CP{How can we measure or quantify what it means for performance to be ``as good as''?} \XL{We use the rMSE of the prediction to measure the performance.}


% For the estimation of the propensity score $p^*$, a natural approach is assume a similar spatial mixed effect model for the well depth and estimate the conditional density of the observed well depth. However, in our nitrate example, this approach led to unstable inverse weights in the augmentation term in \eqref{eq:pseudo_outcome}. To remedy the problem, we adopt recent methods \citet{fong2018covariate} that directly estimate the weights (instead of the inverse of the estimated conditional density) to balance the covariates across the range of a continuous treatment. The method can be conveniently implemented using R package \texttt{WeightIt} \citep{greifer2025weightit}. We add the kriging estimation of $U_i$ obtained in Step \ref{step:kriging} as additional covariate to balance. 



\subsection{Support Vector Machines Under Spatial Dependence} \label{subsec:SVM}
In this paper, we consider an SVM approach to solve for $\theta^*$, which was shown to be a solution to a risk minimization problem in Lemma \ref{lemma:risk_db}. Consider a Gaussian kernel  $\mathcal{K}[(x,u),(x^{'},u^{'})] = \exp\left\{(\|x-x^\prime\|_2^2 + \|u - u^\prime\|_2^2)/\gamma_n^2\right\}$ with bandwidth $\gamma_n$. Let $\mathcal{H}_\mathcal{K}$ denote the corresponding reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS). The representer theorem \citep{KW1970,SHS2001} states that the estimated policy can be written as $\widetilde\theta(x,u) = \sum_{j=1}^n \widehat\eta_j\mathcal{K}[(x,u),(x_j,u_j)]+\widehat b$ where $\widehat\eta_j$ and $\widehat b$ are solutions to the following optimization problem:
\begin{align}
    \widetilde\theta = & \arg\min_{\theta\in\mathcal{H}_\mathcal{K}}\left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \widehat L^{({\delta_n})}(\theta(X_i,\widehat U(S_i)),O_i)+\lambda_n\|\theta\|^2_{\mathcal{H}_\mathcal{K}}\right\} \nonumber\\
    =& \arg\min_{\eta,b}\left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \widehat L^{({\delta_n})}(k_i^\top\eta+b,O_i)+\frac{\lambda_n}{2}\eta^\top K\eta\right\}. \label{eq:objective function} 
\end{align}
Here, $\|\theta\|_{\mathcal{H}_\mathcal{K}}$ is a seminorm of $\theta$ in $\mathcal{H}_\mathcal{K}$, $\lambda_n$ is a regularization parameter, $\mathbf{\eta} = ( \eta_1,\ldots,\eta_n )^\top \in \R^{n}$, $k_i = \big[ \mathcal{K}\{(x_i,u_i),(x_1,u_1)\} , \ldots, \mathcal{K}\{(x_i,u_i),(x_n,u_n) \big]^\top \in \R^{n}$ for $i=1,\ldots,n$, and $K = \big[ k_1,\ldots,k_n \big] \in \R^{n \times n}$. An important distinction between existing works that use SVMs to estimate policy functions \citep{chen2022policy,park2024minimum} and our SVM in \eqref{eq:objective function} is that our SVM's predictor contains $\widehat U(S_i)$, which is obtained by residual kriging from \eqref{eq:kriging}. This effectively forces the predicting variable $(X_i,\widehat U(S_i))$ to be non-i.i.d., breaking the standard assumptions for SVM \citep{christmann2008support}. In Section \ref{sec:theory}, we show how the dependence from $\widehat U(S_i)$ can affect the excess risk of the estimated policy, thereby providing a theoretical comparison between existing results on SVM and our result.


The optimization problem in \eqref{eq:objective function} is nonconvex, but this particular nonconvex function can be efficiently solved; see Section \ref{sec:dc_algorithm} for details. Also, we winsorize the estimated policy $\widetilde{\theta}$ from \ref{eq:objective function} to satisfy support restriction $[a_L,a_U]$ of the policy, i.e.,
$\widehat{\theta} 
 = \widetilde{\theta} \ind\{ \widetilde{\theta}  \in [a_L,a_U] \} + a_U\ind ( \widetilde{\theta}  > a_U )+ a_L\ind ( \widetilde{\theta}  < a_L )$. Investigators can then use the winsorized policy $\widehat{\theta}$ as an estimator of the optimal policy $\theta^*$. 
 

 % \begin{remark}[Dependence of $U$ in SVM]
 %     % First, since $U_i$ is unknown, we would substitute $U_i$ in \eqref{eq:objective function} by the kriging estimator $\widehat\E[U_i|\textbf{U}_{-i}]$ defined in Step \ref{step:kriging} below.  Thus, the function class is the RKHS on $(X_i,\widehat\E[U_i|\textbf{U}_{-i}])$.
 %     The predicting variable $(X_i,\widehat U(S_i))$ does not satisfies the i.i.d. assumption in the standard SVM estimator. In  Section \ref{sec:theory}, we show how the dependence in $\widehat U(S_i)$ affected the estimated policy in terms of the excess risk.
 % \end{remark}

For instance, if an investigator prefers a simple, linear policy, the investigator can replace the RKHS with a space of linear functions, i.e., $\big\{ \beta^\top x \big| \beta \in \R^{\text{dim}(x)} \big\}$, and minimize with respect to the new function space. While the estimated policy may be more interpretable under a linear space, it may have a higher excess risk bound than the policy estimated under RKHS if the true policy is not well-approximated by the space of linear functions; Fourth,
 %     The difference of convex functions (DC) algorithm \citep{DCalgorithm} can be used to efficiently solve \eqref{eq:objective function} and the computational details are in Section \ref{sec:dc_algorithm} in the Supplemental Materials.
 % \end{remark}
   
% \CP{Reviewed! All good}
 \begin{remark}[Choosing Tuning Parameters for SVM] There are three tuning parameters $(\gamma_n, \lambda_n, \delta_n)$ in SVM. Roughly speaking,  $\gamma$ controls the bandwidth of the SVM kernel, $\lambda$ regularizes the SVM coefficients $\eta$ in \eqref{eq:objective function}, and bandwidth ${\delta_n}$ controls the bias-variance trade-off resulting from the surrogate kernel. While there are theoretically optimal choices for these parameters based on rates  (see Theorem \ref{thm:excess_risk} below), in practice, we recommend using cross-validation to choose them. \XL{I think it is hard to. Most people use similar neighbor-excluding strategy to do cross fitting. But we simply split the data. But since $g(S)$ is a fixed function. The data are actually dependent. Sometimes, they use different data generative model and data analysis model for spatial data analysis}For example, %to select $\gamma_n$, $\lambda_n$, and $\delta_n$ 
 for a grid of 
 $(\gamma,\lambda,\delta_n)$, we can find the value that maximizes the cross-validated Matthews correlation coefficient (MCC) \citep{MCC}, 
which is a measure for evaluating the performance of estimated policies by reframing it as a binary classification task at a given threshold $\mathcal{T}$. Notably, unlike other metrics, MCC is agnostic to the shape of the loss function that generated the estimated policy function.
% This is because different values of ${\delta_n}$ correspond to different class of loss functions, and the MCC provides as a uniform measure for evaluating the classification performance of the estimated policy. for the binary classification task at the given threshold $\mathcal{T}$. This is because different values of ${\delta_n}$ correspond to different class of loss functions, and the MCC provides as a uniform measure for evaluating the classification performance of the estimated policy.
 % In general, ${\delta_n}$ governs the bias-variance trade-off of the loss function in \eqref{eq:loss_function_complete}. If ${\delta_n}$ is too large, the consistency of the loss function is compromised (see Lemma \ref{lemma:risk_db}). Conversely, if ${\delta_n}$ is too small, the augmentation term in \eqref{eq:pseudo_outcome} becomes excessively large, driven by sparse observations near the target treatment level. 
 % \CP{I think this last point is somewhat related to the comment near the first remark, and it might be better to move the discussion of ${\delta_n}$ there.
    
 \end{remark}

\section{Technical Results}\label{sec:theory}

\subsection{Excess Risk}
In this section, we study the performance of the estimated policy $\widehat\theta$ from the previous section. Building upon the spatial mixed effect model for outcome regression \eqref{eq:spatial_mixed_effect_model}, our theoretical analysis assumes the following generative model of $(Y_i, A_i, X_i, S_i, U_i)$:
\begin{gather}
    Y_i = f(A_i,X_i) + U(S_i) + \epsilon_i, \label{eq:data_generative_model_1}\\
    A_i = \E[A_i|X_i,U(S_i)]+\xi_i, \label{eq:data_generative_model_2} \\
  U(\cdot)\sim \mathrm{GP}(0,C(\cdot,\cdot|\psi^*)), \  \psi^*\in \Psi_{\text{Mat\'ern}}\subset \R^3.
\end{gather}
\XL{Yes, mostly only used on supplement, maybe we can remove them}
%The model assumes that the spatial coordinates $S_i$ influence the observed treatment $A_i$ and the outcome $Y_i$ through the same Gaussian process $U(S_i)$. 
We make two remarks. First, the above model is compatible with the causal DAG in Figure \ref{fig:causal_DAG}(a). Second, the observed covariates $X_i$ are i.i.d. \XL{No, we do not assume $X_i$ to be i.i.d. I think it is easy to make people confused but this data generative model is only for the theoretical analysis} and the dependence between $Y_i$ and $A_i$ arises due to the Gaussian process discussed in Section \ref{sec:nuisance}. 
%$U(S_i)$; 
%In particular, from Section \ref{sec:nuisance}, $U(\cdot)$ is assumed to have Mat\'ern covariance function \eqref{eq:matern} and $\mathbf{C}_n(\psi^*)$ is the covariance matrix with entries $(U(S_i), U(S_j)) = C(S_i,S_j;\psi^*)$. %We assume that the covariance parameter $\psi^*\in \Psi_{\text{Mat\'ern}}\subset \R^3$ and 

We make the following assumptions about the estimated nuisance functions $\widehat\mu,\widehat p$, and the true nuisance functions $\mu^*,p^*$. 

 \begin{enumerate}[label=(C\arabic*)]
     \item \label{condition:excess_risk:smooth} (Regularity Conditions on Nuisance Functions) $\widehat\mu,\mu^*,\widehat p,p^*$ are uniformly continuously differentiable with respect to the treatment level $a\in\mathcal{A}$, and the covariates $(X_i,U_i)$\XL{density function is a smooth w.r.t. $X_i,U_i$. Yes, it might not hold for covariate balancing but I think it is necessary for doubly robust properties}. Also,
     %\item \label{condition:excess_risk:boundedness} (Boundedness) 
     there exists a constant $C^\prime>0$ such that $\widehat\mu,\mu^*, \widehat p, p$ and their first-order derivatives \XL{with respect to $(a,x,u)$}are uniformly bounded by $C^\prime$. 
     \item \label{condition:excess_risk:overlap}(Strict Overlap) There exists a constant $c^\prime >0$ such that
     $\widehat p,p^*$ and their first order-derivative are uniformly bounded below by $c^\prime$.  
     \item \label{condition:excess_risk:nuisance}(Consistency of Nuisance Estimation) Let $r_{p,n},r_{\mu,n}$ and $\rho_n$ be sequences of real numbers, which are $o(1)$ as $n\rightarrow\infty$. We assume $\|\widehat{p} - p^*\|_{P,2} \leq r_{p,n}$ \XL{integrate over $a$} and $\|\widehat{\mu} - \mu^*\|_{P,2} \leq r_{\mu,n}$ with probability no less than $1 - \rho_n$, where $\|\cdot\|_{P,2}$ denotes the $L_2(P)$ norm.
     % Then, we have $\widehat p - p^* \leq r_{p,n}$, $\widehat \mu - \mu^* \leq r_{\mu,n}$ with probability greater than $1-\rho_n$.
     % \CP{Need to specify the metric for convergence e.g. L2?}
     \item \label{condition:excess_risk:VC-type} (VC-Type Estimators of Nuisance Functions) The estimators of the outcome regression model and the generalized propensity score belong to VC-type function classes (see \citet{kosorok2008introduction} for details).\XL{whether a smooth function class depends on smoothness and dimension.}
 \end{enumerate}
%Assumption \ref{condition:excess_risk:VC-type} ensures that the function class used to estimate the nuisance parameters is not overly complex, which is necessary to establish uniform convergence rates (e.g., Glivenko-Cantelli or Donsker properties, see \citet{kosorok2008introduction} for details). 
Assumption \ref{condition:excess_risk:VC-type} can be relaxed if the data can be split to independent or weakly dependent subsets \citep{chernozhukov2018double,colangelo2025double}; see Section \ref{sec:cross-fitting} and Theorem \ref{thm:excess_risk_cross_fitting} for more details.
% use modern machine learning method to estimate the nuisance function. The excess risk corresponds to cross-fitting is illustrated in Theorem \ref{thm:excess_risk_cross_fitting}.

 
% \textcolor{red}{Connect to the version of cross-fitting, which can relax Assumption \ref{condition:excess_risk:VC-type}}.
% \textcolor{red}{What Assumption \ref{condition:excess_risk:VC-type} actually mean, if we list some examples and explanation. Can VC-dimension increase with the sample size?}% Assumption \ref{condition:excess_risk:VC-type}
 
\CP{maybe $\kappa_n$?}\XL{I am OK with that though I use $\sigma_{\epsilon}$ as the SD of $\epsilon$. Also $\kappa$ has been used for the upper bound for the kernel function and its derivative}
Let $\sigma_{n} = \sup_{\psi\in \Psi_{\text{Mat\'ern}}}\|\mathbf{C}_n(\psi)\|_2$ as the supremum of the spectral norm of the covariance matrix $\mathbf{C}_n(\psi^*)$ of the Gaussian process with entries $(U(S_i), U(S_j)) = C(S_i,S_j;\psi^*)$ and $d$ be the dimension of $(X_i,U(S_i))$. Theorem \ref{thm:excess_risk} characterizes the excess risk of $\widehat\theta$, a common metric of performance in this literature (e.g. \citet{Zhao2012, Dias2018, KitagawaTetenov2018}).

% \CP{How should I understand $R(\theta)$ in Lemma \ref{lemma:risk_db} when $X$ is a fixed object?} \XL{Now I change it to i.i.d.}
 \XL{will unify all to Theorem/Proposition}
 \begin{theorem}[Excess Risk Bound of SVM Estimator] \label{thm:excess_risk}
     Suppose Assumptions \ref{condition:monotonicity} and \ref{condition:excess_risk:smooth} - \ref{condition:excess_risk:VC-type} hold. In addition, suppose that $\theta^*$ in \eqref{eq:identification} belongs to a Besov space on $\R^{d+1}$ with smoothness parameter $\beta>0$, i.e., $\mathcal{B}_{1,\infty}^\beta(\R^{d+1}) = \{f\in L_\infty(\R^{d+1})|\sup_{t>0}t^{-\beta\{\omega_{r,L_1(\R^{d+1})}(f,t)\}}\}$ where $\omega_r$ is the modulus of continuity of order $r$. If the surrogate kernel bandwidth $\delta_n$ decays no faster than $n^{-1}$, for any $p\in(0,1)$, $\tau>0$, we have the following excess risk bound for $\widehat\theta$ with probability $1-32e^{-\tau}-\rho_n$:
\begin{align}
       R(\widehat\theta)-R\left(\theta^*\right)\leq &c_1 \lambda_n\gamma_n^{-d} + c_2\gamma_n^{\beta}+ \sqrt{\frac{ c_3\gamma_n^{-2}\lambda_n^{-1}\sigma_n\tau}{n}} + \sqrt{\frac{ c_4\gamma_n^{-2}\lambda_n^{-1}\sigma_n\ln(n)}{n}} \nonumber\\
   & \qquad + c_5\left(\gamma_n^{-(d+2)}\lambda_n^{-(p+1)}n^{-1}\sigma_n\right)^{\frac{1}{2+2p}} + c_6n^{-1}\nonumber\\
       & \qquad\qquad + c_7r_{\mu,n}r_{p,n} +  c_8{\delta_n} \label{eq:excess_risk}
    \end{align}   
Here, $c_1,\ldots,c_8$ are constants that are independent of $n$. 
\end{theorem}
\CP{In the first version of the MRTP paper, we presented only the simplified version (corresponding to $O_P\left((\sigma_n^{-1}n)^{-\frac{\beta}{3\beta + 2d + 2}}\right)+O_P(r_{\mu,n}r_{p,n} )$). However, the reviewers requested to bring back \eqref{eq:excess_risk}. From my understanding, the OTR community tends to be obsessive about the explicit form...}
\XL{$\tau$ can be any arbitrarily chosen positive number, and I have added for every Theorem}
\noindent To better interpret the result, suppose the Gaussian kernel bandwidth parameter $\gamma_n$, the regularization parameter $\lambda_n$, and the surrogate kernel bandwidth $\delta_n$ are chosen with rates $\lambda_n \propto (\sigma_n^{-1}n)^{-\frac{d+\beta}{3\beta + 2d + 2}}$, $\gamma_n\propto (\sigma_n^{-1}n)^{-\frac{1}{3\beta + 2d + 2}}$, and $\delta_n\propto n^{-1}$. Under these choices, the bound in \eqref{eq:excess_risk} reduces to 
$$R(\widehat\theta)-R\left(\theta^*\right)=O_P\left((\sigma_n^{-1}n)^{-\frac{\beta}{3\beta + 2d + 2}}\right)+O_P(r_{\mu,n}r_{p,n} )$$

% \textcolor{red}{Maybe put in discussion: why we have the rate $n^{-1/3}$ because the concentration inequality of dependent random variable. But we might mention it is due to spatial correlation.}
The first term corresponds to the convergence rate of the SVM using Gaussian kernels, which depends on the smoothness of the optimal policy $\theta^*$ (parameterized by $\beta$), the dependence of the underlying Gaussian process $U(S_i)$ (parameterized by $\sigma_n$). In the bound, $\sigma_n$ quantifies the impact of the spatial dependence in $U(S_i)$ on the convergence rate, a factor that has not appeared in prior literature. For standard GP covariance kernels defined over irregular locations in $\mathbb{R}^2$, $\sigma_n$ would be bounded under increasing domain asymptotics if all locations are separated by a minimum distance. Under this condition, our bound is, up to a scaling factor, identical to that of an SVM in i.i.d. settings\XL{, i.e., $O_p(n^{-\beta/(3\beta+2d+2)})$}. We briefly remark that recent theoretical analyses of one-layer neural networks in the context of spatially dependent data have also pointed out the non-trivial role of $\sigma_n$ in the convergence rate \citep{zhan2024neural} \XL{This is correct}.

If the true policy $\theta^*$ is very smooth (i.e., $\beta\rightarrow \infty$ with fixed $d$) and the locations of observations are separated by a minimum distance (i.e., $\sigma_n$ remain bounded), the first term approaches the rate $O_P(n^{-\frac{1}{3}})$. In related work employing SVM in optimal policy learning, \citet{park2024minimum} achieves a rate of $O_p(n^{-1/2})$ in the binary treatment settings, and \citet{chen2016personalized} achieves a rate of $O_p(n^{-1/4})$ in the continuous treatment setting. Our rate $O_p(n^{-1/3})$ is slower than the $O_p(n^{-1/2})$ in \citet{park2024minimum} due to a surrogate kernel, which is necessary to deal with a continuous treatment in our setting. However, our rate is faster than the $O_p(n^{-1/4})$ in \citet{chen2016personalized}, who also used a surrogate kernel to deal with a continuous treatment. The difference in rates is attributable to the fact that our loss function \eqref{eq:loss_function_complete} is defined in terms of an integral over the surrogate kernel, which effectively smooths the loss function and mitigates the variance inflation typically associated with surrogate kernels. The only requirement to achieve our faster rate is that $\delta_n$ converges to zero no faster than $n^{-1}$. 

The second term $O_P( r_{p,n} r_{\mu,n})$
represents error contribution from the estimated nuisance functions. This term captures the second-order bias characteristic of the doubly robust loss function, a phenomenon resulting from its careful design of the loss function in \eqref{eq:loss_function_complete}. %Under the spatial mixed effect model \eqref{eq:spatial_mixed_effect_model}, both the nonparametric estimation error from Step \ref{step:regression}, and the kriging estimation error from Step \ref{step:kriging} contribute to the nuisance estimation error. 

% Notably, in scenarios where a larger ${\delta_n}$ is necessary to manage the bias-variance trade-off, the factor ${\delta_n} r_{\mu,n}$ in the first term would dominate $O_P( r_{p,n} r_{\mu,n})$, and the surrogate kernel's approximation error becomes the primary component of the excess risk.



% thereby weakening the double robustness property.

% If the sample size is small or the optimal policy differs substantially from the assigned treatment, a larger ${\delta_n}$ is necessary to manage the bias-variance trade-off inherent in \eqref{eq:loss_function_complete}. Under these conditions, the error rate $O_P({\delta_n} r_{\mu,n})$ would dominate the doubly robust bias term $O_P( r_{p,n} r_{\mu,n})$, thereby weakening the double robustness property. 

% If we further choose ${\delta_n}\propto (\sigma_n)^{-\frac{\beta}{3\beta +d}}$, the bound in \eqref{eq:excess_risk} becomes$$R(\widehat\theta)-R\left(\theta^*\right)=O_P\left((\sigma_n^{-1} n)^{-\frac{\beta}{3\beta + d}}\right)+O_P(r_{\mu,n}r_{p,n})$$

\subsection{Cross-Fitting} \label{sec:cross-fitting}

%Machine learning method provide advantages for estimating the nuisance parameters $\mu^*$ and $p^*$ but violate the complexity requirements in Assumption \ref{condition:excess_risk:VC-type}. 
A popular technique to avoid the complexity Assumption \ref{condition:excess_risk:VC-type} is cross fitting, wherein nuisance functions are estimated and the loss function \eqref{eq:loss_function_complete} is constructed using independent data splits.
While independent data splitting may not always be feasible in spatial datasets, we nevertheless follow \citet{gilbert2021causal} and consider a setup where $(Y, A, X, S)$ is identically distributed and exhibits spatial dependence according to a locally covariance random field. Under this setup, Bernstein blocking can be employed to obtain independent subsets of a spatial dataset, which then enables cross-fitting and relaxes Assumption \ref{condition:excess_risk:VC-type} to accommodate flexible machine learning estimators. Theorem \ref{thm:excess_risk_cross_fitting} extends our theoretical analysis to this cross-fitting regime.

 \begin{theorem}[Excess Risk Bound of Cross-Fitting Estimator] \label{thm:excess_risk_cross_fitting}
     Suppose Assumptions \ref{condition:monotonicity} and \ref{condition:excess_risk:smooth} - \ref{condition:excess_risk:nuisance} hold. \XL{Suppose the observations are distributed identically and according to a locally covariant spatial random field, and the nuisance estimators $\widehat\mu,\widehat p$ and the target estimator $\widehat\theta$ are estimated using independent subsets obtained via Bernstein blocking (see Section 5.2 of \citet{gilbert2021causal} for details).}. In addition, suppose that $\theta^*$ in \eqref{eq:identification} belongs to a Besov space on $\R^{d+1}$ with smoothness parameter $\beta>0$, i.e., $\mathcal{B}_{1,\infty}^\beta(\R^{d+1}) = \{f\in L_\infty(\R^{d+1})|\sup_{t>0}t^{-\beta\{\omega_{r,L_1(\R^{d+1})}(f,t)\}}\}$ where $\omega_r$ is the modulus of continuity of order $r$. Then, for any $p\in(0,1)$, $\tau>0$, we have the following excess risk bound for $\widehat\theta$ with probability $1-32e^{-\tau}-\rho_n$:
\begin{align}
       R(\widehat\theta)-R\left(\theta^*\right) 
       \leq & c_1 \lambda_n\gamma_n^{-d} + c_2\gamma_n^{\beta}+ \sqrt{\frac{ c_3\gamma_n^{-2}\lambda_n^{-1}\sigma_n\tau}{n}} + c_4\left(\gamma_n^{-(d+2)}\lambda_n^{-(p+1)}n^{-1}\sigma_n\right)^{\frac{1}{2+2p}} + c_5r_{\mu,n}r_{p,n} + c_6{\delta_n}. \label{eq:excess_risk_cross_fitting}
    \end{align}       
Here, $c_1,\ldots,c_6$ are constants that are independent of $n$. 
\XL{Very similar to previous Theorem, a few terms gone including the $\ln(n)$ term. Because we have independent sample and do not inquire union bound over the space of nuisance function.}
\end{theorem}

The excess risk bound in \eqref{eq:excess_risk_cross_fitting} eliminates two higher-order terms $c_4\gamma_n^{-2}\lambda_n^{-1}\sigma_n\ln(n)/n+c_6n^{-1}$ present in \eqref{eq:excess_risk}. Also, if we choose tuning parameters \XL{$\lambda_n \propto (\sigma_n^{-1}n)^{-\frac{d+\beta}{3\beta + 2d + 2}}$, $\gamma_n\propto (\sigma_n^{-1}n)^{-\frac{1}{3\beta + 2d + 2}}$, and $\delta_n\propto n^{-1}$, the upper bound reduces to 
$$R(\widehat\theta)-R\left(\theta^*\right)=O_P\left((\sigma_n^{-1}n)^{-\frac{\beta}{3\beta + 2d + 2}}\right)+O_P(r_{\mu,n}r_{p,n} )$$}

\XL{The choice of the tunning parameters and the resulting bound are the same as the previous one.}

\XL{The effect of dependence in all in $\sigma_n$, even if it is slightly weaker. It is reflected in $\sigma_n$. Also, in OLS, neural network, the rate are all similar to i.i.d. setting. So slightly weakening the dependence assumption does not change the risk. Everything is just we have an additionally $\sigma_n$ in the concentrational inequality because of the dependence of Gaussian process.}
\subsection{Parametric Policy Functions}

Although we recommend the SVM estimator of the policy function $\theta^*$, which lies inside a nonparametric RKHS, our framework can easily accommodate function classes other than RKHS. In particular, if a simple, interpretable policy function is desired, the investigator can replace the RKHS with a parametric function class, i.e., $\{\theta(x,u;\beta):\beta\in \R^{d}\}$, where $d$ is the dimension of $(X_i,\widehat U(S_i))$, and minimize the empirical loss with the new function space. Theorem \ref{thm:excess_risk_parametric} extends our theoretical analysis to this parametric policy class.

\begin{theorem}[Excess Risk Bound of Parametric Estimator] \label{thm:excess_risk_parametric}
     Suppose Assumptions \ref{condition:monotonicity} and \ref{condition:excess_risk:smooth} - \ref{condition:excess_risk:VC-type} hold. In addition, suppose that $\theta^*$ in \eqref{eq:identification} belongs to a parametric space $\{\beta^\top(x,u):\beta\in \R^{d+1},\|\beta\|_\infty<C_\beta\}$. Then, for $\tau>0$,we have the following excess risk bound for $\widehat\theta$ with probability $1-32e^{-\tau}-\rho_n$:
\begin{align}
       R(\widehat\theta)-R\left(\theta^*\right)\leq &  \sqrt{\frac{c_1\sigma_n\tau}{n}} + \sqrt{\frac{ c_2\sigma_n\ln(n)}{n}}  + c_3n^{-1}+ c_4r_{\mu,n}r_{p,n} + c_5{\delta_n}\label{eq:excess_risk_parametric}
    \end{align} 
Here, $c_1,\ldots,c_5$ are constants that are independent of $n$. 
\end{theorem}

\section{Simulation Study}\label{sec:simulation}
We evaluate the finite-sample performance of our method through a small simulation study. Consider a $50\times 50$ uniform grid on $[0,1]\times [0,1]$\XL{, i.e., 2500 sampling locations}. For every location on the grid, we generate a four-dimensional covariate $X = (W_1,W_2,W_3,W_4)$ and a one-dimensional unmeasured covariate $U$ by (i) generating five independent Gaussian processes with a Mat\'ern covariance function whose variance equals 0.25, range equals 0.2, and smoothness parameter equals 0.5; (ii) letting three of the generated Gaussian processes be $(W_1,W_2)$ and $U$; and (iii) obtaining $W_3,W_4$ by transforming the other two Gaussian processes to be \XL{four-level }categorical variables defined by their quartiles. \XL{The observed treatment is generated via $A = X\beta + 0.5 U + \epsilon$ where $\beta$ is a finite-dimensional parameter, $\epsilon$ are i.i.d. normally distributed random error; the observed outcome is generated via $Y = -(0.5 + 0.1X3 +0.1X4) A + f(X)+U +\xi$ where where $f(\cdot)$ is a nonlinear function of the observed covariates, $\epsilon$ are i.i.d. normally distributed random error.} The target threshold $\mathcal{T}$ is set to be 40-th, 50-th, and 60-th percentiles of the observed outcomes $Y$.
% The simulation settings vary among the following scenarios: (i) the outcome regression model is linear or nonlinear; (ii) the target threshold is set to the 40th, 50th, and 60th percentiles of the observed outcome; and (iii) the function class of the policy is a space of linear functions or an RKHS. 

After generating the data, we split the data into a training set (70\%) and a test set (30\%). We then compare the performance of the ``classic'' indirect method that ignores spatial confounding in \citet{chakraborty2013statistical}, the indirect method that estimates $\widehat\mu$ in \eqref{eq:or_estimation} , and the direct method introduced in Section \ref{sec:direct_method}, which are all evaluated on the test set. We repeat this entire process 30 times and, therefore, obtain 75000 policy predictions for each method and threshold \XL{2500 locations corresponds to 2500 policies, 30 repetitions give us 30 x 2500  = 75000 policy predictions}.

Figure \ref{fig:sim_boxplot} shows the absolute deviations between the estimated and the true policy. The direct approach shows the best performance, where its absolute deviation between the predicted and the true policies is generally the smallest across all threshold values $\mathcal{T}$.

\section{Designing Nitrate Mitigation Policy in Wisconsin}\label{sec:application}

\subsection{Background on Environmental Policy}

Nitrate is the most widespread groundwater contaminant in Wisconsin \citep{saffigna1977nitrate,schubert1999public,mathewson2020health}, affecting both public and private water wells. 
% Excess nitrate of drinking water increases the risk of certain cancers and impacts fetal development during pregnancy \citep{Ward2005, USEPA1995a}.
To protect against health risks imposed by nitrate contaminants, the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency established the drinking water standard for nitrate at 10 mg/L \citep{USEPA1995a}. Exceedance of 5 mg/L nitrate is another critical threshold in Wisconsin as concentrations above 5 mg/L trigger increased monitoring requirements and a health effects statement for public water supply systems \citep{nitrate2025}. 
%The 5 mg/L threshold is a signal to begin source water protection planning; waiting until 10 mg/L is exceeded is too late \citep{nitrate2025}. 
Between 2020 and 2024, approximately 6\% of nitrate measurements in Wisconsin exceeded the 10 mg/L threshold, and 20\% exceeded the 5 mg/L threshold. The exceedance rates are spatially heterogeneous, with some counties showing significantly higher rates (see Figure \ref{fig:exceedance_rate}). 

%To obtain a safe water supply, 
Any public water system that has nitrate levels above 10 mg/L is in violation of  federal law and must either replace the well or install approved water treatment systems \citep{CFR2016}. For private wells, if their nitrate levels exceed 10 mg/L, well owners of nitrate-contaminated private wells can qualify for the new American Rescue Plan Act (ARPA)'s  Well Compensation and Well Abandonment Grant Program \citep{WIDNR_ARPA_2022}. The cost of well replacement is mainly determined by the depth of the wells drilled and since October 2022, more than 10 million U.S. dollars has been allocated to replace private wells, and more than 40 million U.S. dollars has been allocated to replace public wells in Wisconsin. 
%Unfortunately, due to the large number of private wells in Wisconsin, the estimated cost to replace all non-compliant private wells is over 420 million dollars. 
In this section, we use our method to design a safe, cost-effective nitrate policy given the threshold $\mathcal{T}=$ 5 mg/L in Wisconsin. The policy for $\mathcal{T}=$ 10 mg/L is in the Supplementary Material. \XL{Add Plot}

We consider all Wisconsin groundwater wells with validated nitrate measurements between 2020 and 2024; the database of all groundwater wells can be found from.... For each well $i$, we define the observed outcome, $Y_i$, as the log-transformed nitrate measurement obtained between 2020 and 2024; we remark that groundwater nitrate levels are generally stable over time in both private and public wells. 
Also for each well $i$, we let $A_i$ be its well depth, which is defined as ...

The primary contributors to groundwater nitrate are agricultural activities, specifically the application of synthetic fertilizers and manure from concentrated animal feeding operations (CAFOs) \citep{Nolan2002, Hudak2000, Spalding1993, Sobota2013, Sabo2018}. Following the application, nitrate dissolves in water and percolates through the soil and bedrock into the underlying aquifer. Motivated by this transport mechanism, we define $X_i$ to include both nitrate sources (land use, CAFOs) and the hydrogeological features affecting nitrate transport (soil drainage, precipitation, and static water level). Figure \ref{fig:nitrate_visualization} provides a spatial visualization of the dataset and Section \ref{sec} of the Supplementary Materials contains additional details.



\subsection{Main Results}

\noindent The right panel of Figure \ref{fig:policy_and_covariates} illustrates the estimated safe, cost-effective nitrate policy relative to a 5 mg/L threshold. Visually, the required minimum well depths are greater in locations with CAFOs and intensive agricultural activity. We also assessed  this statistically by projecting the estimated policy onto the spatial predictors via ordinary least squares (OLS); the required well depth is positively associated with the CAFO variable ($p\text{-value} \le 2e^{-16}$), and all agricultural land uses exhibit a larger marginal effect relative to the baseline of ``Forest'' (Figure \ref{fig:policy_and_covariates}, bottom-left). These findings align with the understanding that synthetic fertilizers and manure from CAFOs are primary contributors of nitrogen (N), which oxidizes into nitrate in the soil. 

Interestingly, among different crop types, we find that the marginal mean of the required well depth is largest for vegetables, followed by corn, and finally soybeans. The high requirement for vegetables is likely driven by potatoes, a dominant crop in Wisconsin. Potatoes possess shallow root systems and low nitrogen use efficiency, often necessitating higher fertilizer application rates than corn to ensure uptake \citep{laboski2012nutrient}. While corn is also a nutrient-intensive crop, its deeper root system allows for slightly more efficient capture of inputs compared to potatoes \citep{stalham2007effects}. In contrast, soybeans require the lowest level of intervention; as legumes, they maintain a symbiotic relationship with bacteria that fix atmospheric nitrogen, minimizing the need for external fertilizers \citep{salvagiotti2008nitrogen}. 
% \CP{Do we need references to justify these, or is it unnecessary?} \XL{Done}

Also, the recommended well depth is significantly influenced by soil drainage and precipitation. As shown in the top-left panel of Figure \ref{fig:policy_and_covariates}, with the exception of the extreme categories (very poorly and excessively drained), the required well depth increases monotonically with soil drainage levels. We also find that precipitation has a positive linear effect when we project the estimated policy on precipitation via OLS
($p\text{-value} \le 2e^{-16}$), indicating that areas with higher rainfall require deeper wells to access safe water. This relationship is driven by the role of precipitation as the primary transport mechanism for nitrate; higher rainfall and well-drained soil better mobilize surface nitrates and drive the contaminant deeper into the aquifer.

\subsection{Comparison to Non-Spatial Method}

To show the importance of accounting for spatial confounding and dependence in a real dataset, we compared the performance of our proposed method against a non-spatial method that relies on assumptions of no unmeasured confounding and i.i.d. observations in the nitrate dataset. Specifically, the non-spatial method solves the optimization problem in \eqref{eq:identification} without $S$ and by plugging in an estimated value of $\mu^*$. 

We randomly partitioned our dataset into training (70\%) and test (30\%) sets, using the training data to estimate the policy. Table \ref{tab:model_performance} shows the classification performance metrics. Across all measures, our approach consistently outperforms the non-spatial indirect method. 

Figure \ref{fig:indirect_inference} visually illustrates the policy estimated via the non-spatial method and its projection onto measured covariates. The left panel shows the marginal effects of various land use categories and drainage levels. While the non-spatial method correctly identifies the general increase in required well depth associated with agricultural activities, it lacks the resolution to distinguish between specific crop types. Specifically, the confidence intervals for corn and soybeans overlap and are barely separated from the confidence interval for vegetables (potatoes). On the right panel, which displays the marginal effects of soil drainage, the consistent linear trend observed in our proposed method is less apparent in the non-spatial method where the non-spatial method simply shows a sharp increase in between ``somewhat poorly drained'' and ``moderately poorly drained.'' 
We attribute the gap between the two methods to a potential violation of the no unmeasured confounding assumption where the effects of unmeasured spatial confounders are misattributed to the observed predictors. Overall, our method, which incorporates spatial confounding, provides more scientifically consistent and granular recommendations for well depth compared to non-spatial methods.
%We attribute the gap between the two methods to be due to violation of the no unmeasured confounding assumption where the effects of unmeasured spatial confounders are misattributed to the observed predictors. In contrast, our method, which incorporates spatial confounding, mitigates this bias and yields more reliable estimates of the effects of geographical features.

\section{Discussion}\label{sec: discussion}
%Motivated by designing interventions for nitration mitigation in groundwater, 
This paper develops a statistical framework for learning safe, cost-effective policies in the presence of unique challenges posed by environmental observational data, spatial confounding, and spatial dependence. We present identification conditions that use spatial location as a proxy for unmeasured confounders. We then propose a method for estimating the policy based on ERM with a novel, doubly robust loss function. We derive excess risk bounds of the estimated policy accounting for the spatial dependence and use it to estimate a safe, cost-effective policy for nitrate mitigation in Wisconsin. Compared to the non-spatial method, we show that our method is more accurate and can recover scientifically interpretable relationships.

While we focused on continuous interventions, our method generalizes naturally to non-binary, but discrete interventions and we briefly highlight this generalization here. Suppose the range of the treatment is represented by the set of integers $\{a_L,a_L +1,\ldots,a_U\}$. Assuming the monotonicity assumption \ref{condition:monotonicity} holds for these discrete interventions, the safe cost-effective policy defined in \eqref{eq:identification_1} can be estimated through a two step process: (i) finding the minimizer $\tilde\theta$ of the loss function in \eqref{eq:loss_function_complete} where we replace the integral in the loss function with a summation over the discrete set $\mathcal{A}$; (ii) and setting the estimated policy as $\hat\theta = \tilde \theta + 1$. The primary challenge in the discrete case is that the resulting loss function is non-differentiable and consequently, standard gradient-based optimization methods for ERM are inapplicable, necessitating the use of alternative optimization algorithms.


We conclude by discussing the potential future directions of our work. First, from a statistical modeling perspective, our framework imposes three key assumptions: (i) the spatial confounder is additively separable, which is due to the spatial mixed effects model and (ii) the estimated policy with theoretical guarantees is restricted by the kernel of the SVM. % and (iii) the nuisance functions is assumed to be belong to VC-type classes. 
One general future direction would be to relax these constraints to allow more flexible models to enter into the estimated policy. Second, this paper only explored a ``frequentist'' approach to estimating the policy function. An equally interesting avenue for future research is to adapt scalable, nonparametric Bayesian methods to learn a safe, cost-effective spatial policy. 

%for continuous treatments due to the lack of a closed-form solution. A promising avenue for future research is to adapt scalable Bayesian computation techniques to the problem of spatial policy learning.

\section{Additional Details of the Main Paper }\label{sec:additional_details}

\subsection{Examples of Popular Contaminants and Their Regulation Standards }\label{sec:other_threshold}

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Contaminants in Air:} The Clean Air Act \citep{popp2003pollution}, which was last amended in 1990, requires EPA to set National Ambient Air Quality Standards (NAAQS) \citep{bachmann2007will} for six principal pollutants:  carbon monoxide (CO), lead (Pb), nitrogen dioxide ($NO_2$), ozone ($O_3$), sulfur dioxide ($SO_2$), and particulate matter (PM). These standards are designed to mitigate risks to both public health and the environment. The Clean Air Act identifies two types of national ambient air quality standards. Primary standards provide public health protection, including protecting the health of "sensitive" populations such as asthmatics, children, and the elderly. Secondary standards provide public welfare protection, including protection against decreased visibility and damage to animals, crops, vegetation, and buildings. Specific standards are available at \url{https://www.epa.gov/criteria-air-pollutants/naaqs-table}.
    \item \textbf{Contaminants in Water}: EPA sets legal limits on over 90 contaminants including arsenic, radon, PFAS, and various pesticides and herbicides. The legal limit for a contaminant reflects the level that protects human health and that water systems can achieve using the best available technology. The Safe Drinking Water Act (SDWA) \citep{act1974safe} gives individual states the opportunity to set and enforce their own drinking water standards if the standards are at a minimum as stringent as EPA's national standards. Specific standards are available at \url{https://www.epa.gov/dwreginfo/drinking-water-regulations}.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Examples of Safe, Cost-Effective Mitigation Policy of Contaminants} \label{sec:other_application}

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{PM 2.5 and Filter "Pressure Drop"}: In industrial facilities like power plants, PM 2.5 is typically captured using baghouses or electrostatic precipitators \citep{zhao2016slip}. While increasing filter fabric density or precipitator voltage can reduce emissions, these interventions come with a "pressure drop" penalty. Pushing air through denser media requires significantly more electricity to power industrial fans, leading to a law of diminishing returns; removing the final 1\% of particulate matter can require a 20\% increase in energy consumption. Our method can be applied to identify the optimal filter density that balances public health safety with economic and operational viability.
    \item \textbf{PFAS and Location of Industrial Facilities}: Primary sources of PFAS contamination include industrial facilities \citep{park2024statistical}. Since PFAS concentrations typically exhibit a monotonic decrease as distance from the source increases, it is necessary to determine the minimum setback requirements for new industrial developments relative to urban centers. Our method can be applied to determine the optimal, cost-effective distance to ensure public safety while minimizing economic disruption.
\end{itemize}


\subsection{Empirical Assessment of the Monotonic Response Assumption }\label{sec:monotonic_assessment}

Furthermore, we empirically assess the monotonicity assumption \ref{condition:monotonicity}, that the predicted nitrate levels $\widehat{\mu}(a, X, S)$ decrease as well depth $a$ increases. We find that this assumption is rarely violated in our groundwater dataset; where violations do occur, the deviation from monotonicity is negligible. Specifically, we evaluate the variations between adjacent predictions across a fine grid of treatment levels $\{a^{(1)}, \dots, a^{(K)}\}$ spanning the observed range of well depths. For each observation $i$ in the test set, we calculate the local variations:$$\widehat{V}_{i,k} = \widehat{\mu}(a^{(k+1)}, X_i) - \widehat{\mu}(a^{(k)}, X_i,S_i), \quad k = 1, \dots, K-1.$$
To ensure the robustness of our assessment and avoid overfitting, the outcome regression model is estimated on training data (70\% of the dataset) and evaluated on the randomly held-out test data (30\% of the dataset). The validity of the decreasing monotonicity is empirically assessed by two primary metrics. First, we calculate the violation frequency by counting the number of instances where the estimated slope is positive ($\widehat{V} > 0$). Second, we measure the relative magnitude of these violations compared to the total variation of the predicted surface. Let $TV_{i,k} = |\widehat{V}_{i,k}|$ denote the absolute variation. We compute the ratio of the sum of positive (non-monotonic) slopes to the total variation (PTV):$$\text{Relative Magnitude} = \frac{\sum_{i,k} \widehat{V}_{i,k} \cdot \mathbbm{1}(\widehat{V}_{i,k} > 0)}{\sum_{i,k} TV_{i,k}}.$$Overall, our assessment shows that the decreasing monotonicity assumption is highly consistent with the observed data. Under the first assessment, we found that monotonicity is violated in 18.32\% of the tested variations. Under the second assessment, the relative magnitude of these increasing slopes is 6.48\%. In summary, these empirical validations confirm that groundwater nitrate levels generally decrease with well depth in our study area, and any observed deviations are statistically minor and likely attributable to localized estimation noise.

\subsection{Details of Classification Performance Measures
}\label{sec:binary_measure}

For a given policy $\theta$, we define the \textit{true positives} (TP), \textit{true negatives} (TN), \textit{false positives} (FP), and \textit{false negatives} (FN) as follows:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:classification_measures_base}
\begin{aligned}
\text{TP} &= \sum_{i \in \mathcal{D}_{\text{test}}} 1 \{ Y_i > \mathcal{T}, A_i > \theta(X_i,\widehat{U}(S_i)) \} , & \text{TN} &= \sum_{i \in \mathcal{D}_{\text{test}}} 1 \{ Y_i \le \mathcal{T}, A_i \le \theta(X_i,\widehat{U}(S_i)) \} , \\
\text{FP} &= \sum_{i \in \mathcal{D}_{\text{test}}} 1 \{ Y_i \le \mathcal{T}, A_i > \theta(X_i,\widehat{U}(S_i)) \} , & \text{FN} &= \sum_{i \in \mathcal{D}_{\text{test}}} 1 \{ Y_i > \mathcal{T}, A_i \le \theta(X_i,\widehat{U}(S_i)) \} .
\end{aligned} 
\end{equation}
Given these definitions, we use the following classification performance measures: accuracy, two-sided F1 score, and the Matthews correlation coefficient (MCC) \citep{MCC}  which are defined as follows:
\begin{gather*}
\text{Accuracy} = \frac{\text{TP} + \text{TN}}{\text{TP} + \text{TN} + \text{FP} + \text{FN}} , \quad \text{F1} = \frac{2\text{TP}}{2\text{TP} + \text{FP} + \text{FN}} + \frac{2\text{TN}}{2\text{TN} + \text{FP} + \text{FN}} , \\
\text{MCC} = \frac{\text{TP} \times \text{TN} - \text{FP} \times \text{FN}}{\{(\text{TP} + \text{FP}) \times (\text{TP} + \text{FN}) \times (\text{TN} + \text{FP}) \times (\text{TN} + \text{FN})\}^{1/2}} .
\end{gather*}
The usual F1 score does not use true negatives in its score, i.e., $2\text{TP}/(2\text{TP}+\text{FP}+\text{FN})$, and is sensitive to the definition of a positive label. For example, if we were to define the positive label as the opposite of the definition in equation \eqref{eq:classification_measures_base}, i.e., positive label if $Y_i \le \mathcal{T}$, the F1 score changes. To avoid this, we consider the two-sided F1 score, the average of the usual F1 score and the ``opposite'' F1 score, $2\text{TN}/(2\text{TN}+\text{FP}+\text{FN})$.

\section{Details of Data Sources and Preprocessing}\label{sec:data source}

\subsection{Nitrate Measurement}

The Wisconsin Department of Natural Resources (DNR) maintains the Groundwater Retrieval Network (GRN) to record groundwater quality throughout the state of Wisconsin. The Groundwater Retrieval Network (GRN) reports well information and well sample result data from several DNR databases, including the Public Land Survey System (PLSS) database, the Wisconsin well database, and the sample history of specific groundwater pollutants for specific wells.

\subsubsection{Public Land Survey System}\label{sec:plss}

When the land was first surveyed in Wisconsin, it was divided into a grid as shown in Figure \ref{fig:plss}. Each grid cell represents approximately 36 square miles (the measurements were not always precise due to the instruments the surveyors were using, among other limitations). This grid system is known as the Public Land Survey System (PLSS). Each cell in the grid is identified by a \textit{township} and \textit{range} number. The range number identifies how many cells the property is to the east or west of a starting point. Both eastern and western ranges are possible in Wisconsin. The township number identifies how many cells the property is to north or south of a starting point. Only northern townships are possible in Wisconsin. Each 36-square-mile parcel identified by a township and range number is further divided into 36 \textit{sections}, each section theoretically being 1 square mile or 640 acres. Many parcels of land are smaller than an entire section. They sometimes are the size of a quarter section. Each section is divided into 4 \textit{quarters}, each being 1/4 square miles or 160 acres. An example of a legal description using the PLSS is ``S24, T32N, R18E'' where the township number is 32 north, range number is 18 east, and section number is 24, as shown in Figure \ref{fig:plss}.


\begin{figure}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth]{plss-example.png}
    \caption{PLSS in Wisconsin \citep{widnr_plss_tutorial}.}
    \label{fig:plss}
\end{figure}


\subsubsection{Wisconsin Wells}

\textit{Wisconsin Wells} dataset is the well inventory used by the Groundwater Retrieval Network application. Each well has a unique well ID and the locations of wells are recorded using the PLSS descriptions. The dataset has the geographical information of the wells, including the well bottom depth, the casing depth, the bedrock depth, and the static water level. 

Casing depth is the depth to which the well casing extends below the ground surface, where the casing serves to stabilize the wellbore and prevent contamination from surface or subsurface sources. The static water level refers to the distance from the ground surface to the water surface in a well when the well is at rest. The well bottom depth indicates where the bottom of the well is located. The bedrock depth refers to the distance from the ground surface to the top of the bedrock layer. However, in the center part of Wisconsin where bedrock may be near or at the surface, the bedrock depth is invalid. 
% \begin{figure}
%     \centering
%     \includegraphics[width=1\linewidth]{well.png}
%     \caption{Well geography \CP{Need to address} \textcolor{red}{to xindi: make sure to add citations on where you got this figure in the caption. Also, you need to double-check copyright issues (unless it's owned by the state or federal government)}}
%     \label{fig:well}
% \end{figure}
The static water level and well depth are the two factors that significantly influence how contaminants interact with and affect well water. Figure \ref{fig:staticlevel} and Figure \ref{fig:welldepth} show the static water level and depth of wells in Wisconsin. The well depth is calculated by
$$\text{Well Depth} = \text{Casing Bottom Depth} + \frac{\text{Well Bottom Depth}-\text{Casing Bottom Depth}}{2}.$$

\begin{figure}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{staticlevel.png}
    \caption{Static water (Feet) level of wells in Wisconsin}
    \label{fig:staticlevel}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{welldepth.png}
    \caption{Well depth in Wisconsin}
    \label{fig:welldepth}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Static Water Level}

Static water level is recorded at each nitrate measurement and it is one of the covariates (confounders) in the loss function. However, to estimate the location-specific policy, i.e., the map of the minimum well depth required to meet the threshold, the value of static water level are needed at every PLSS grid cell. We use kriging with a Matern covariance function to interpolate the static water levels. In our dataset, there are multiple wells within single PLSS grid cell and some wells receive multiple measurements. The measurement of static water level can be different from time to time and well to well. Thus, we have multiple different measurements of static water level at each PLSS grid. Kriging effectively reconciles these multiple observations by computing a weighted average that incorporates both local measurements and neighboring data, informed by the estimated spatial dependence structure.

% The dataset also records the usage information of wells. For example, private potable well is used for drinking and household use owned by individuals. Private non-potable well is used for non-drinking purposes like lawn irrigation or livestock use. Municipal community wells serve as drinking water for communities. Different types of wells are designed with distinct standards, features, and regulatory requirements, depending on their purpose, usage, and the populations they serve.




\subsubsection{Nitrate}

The Wisconsin Department of Agriculture, Trade and Consumer Protection (DATCP) performs routine monitoring for nitrate. DATCP's Bureau of Laboratory Services (BLS) analyzes samples for nitrate using modern analytical methods. Targeted monitoring programs are conducted annually. For example, the targeted sampling program and field-edge monitoring program analyze groundwater samples from monitoring wells and private wells installed within or near agriculture fields. The random statewide survey is conducted every 5 or 10 years. Figure \ref{fig:nitrate count} shows the number of measurements (log-scale) that happen at each PLSS section from 2020 to 2024. Figure \ref{fig:nitrate-county} shows the percentage of measurements exceeding 10mg/L nitrate health standard by counties in Wisconsin. 
\begin{figure}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.65\linewidth]{nitrate-count.png}
    \caption{The average number of measurements of groundwater in Wisconsin}
    \label{fig:nitrate count}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.6\linewidth]{nitrate-county.png}
    \caption{Percentage of  nitrate measurement over standard (10 mg/L) by county}
    \label{fig:nitrate-county}
\end{figure}

The measurements can be retrieved from the Groundwater Retrieval Network (GRN), which gives the well information, the concentration amount, and the time of the measurement. Figure \ref{fig:nitrate-spatial} shows the nitrate concentration measurement in groundwater from 2020 to 2024. The groundwater in regions with high nitrate concentration is sampled and measured much more frequently (See Figure \ref{fig:nitrate count}).

    \caption{Ground water nitrate concentration in Wisconsin from 2020 to 2024}
    \label{fig:nitrate-spatial}

\subsection{Spatial Covariates and Preprocessing}

% Nitrate concentrations in groundwater depend on numerous factors such as land use, agricultural fertilizer application practice, hydrological and hydrogeological conditions, soil properties, weather conditions, and livestock manure. 
In this section, we describe the collection and assignment of spatial covariates to each PLSS section (See Section \ref{sec:plss} ) in Wisconsin. 



\subsubsection{Land Cover}

The Cropland Data Layer (CDL) is a data product produced by the National Agricultural Statistics Service of U.S. Department of Agriculture. It provides geo-referenced, high accuracy, 30 or 56-meter resolution, crop-specific cropland land cover information for up to 48 contiguous states in the U.S. from 1997 to the present. We use the \texttt{CropScapeR} package to download CDL data of Wisconsin. 

A PLSS section might have different types of land cover. To address this issue, we select the crop type that dominates the PLSS section as its value of the covariate ``Land Cover''. There are 69 different land cover categories in Wisconsin in total. We combine them into nine different land cover categories based on the similarities of their effect to nitrates. The combining rule is illustrated in Table \ref{tab:land use}. Though the land use is changing through the years, we use the land use in 2023 to construct the covariates. Figure \ref{fig:land-use} shows the mapping of land use categories in Wisconsin.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{l l}
\toprule
\textbf{Combined} & \textbf{Original} \\ \midrule
Undefined & No Data, Barren, Open Water, Idle Cropland \\ \hline
Other Crop & Apples, Watermelons, Cucumbers, Christmas Trees, Pumpkins, Fruits, Dry Beans \\ \hline
Grass & Alfalfa, Other Hay, Pasture, Herbs, Grass Seed, Clover \\ \hline
Cover Crop & Winter Wheat, Oats, Rye, Sorghum, Barley \\ \hline
Forest & Deciduous/Evergreen/Mixed Forest, Herbaceous/Woody  Wetlands, Shrubland\\ \hline
Developed & Developed Low/Medium/High Intensity, Developed/Open Space, \\ \hline
Corn & Corn, Sweet Corn \\ \hline
Soybeans & Soybeans \\ \hline
Vegetables & Potatoes, Carrots, Peas, Sweet Potatoes, Cabbage \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Combination of land use categories.}
\label{tab:land use}
\end{table}
